### Claude (Assistant)

This has real legs. The part you're pointing at ("what has to happen for things to happen") is exactly what Civ-style games skip, and it's the most interesting part of real anthropology. Here's how I'd shape it.

## Core pillar: conditions, not unlocks

There's no tech tree. Each discovery has real preconditions that come together in the simulation, and when they do, it becomes *possible*, not guaranteed.

- **Pottery** needs clay nearby, controlled fire, and a reason to store things. Nomads don't haul heavy pots, so it also needs some settling down.
- **Agriculture** needs wild grasses with the right traits, plus people staying near the same patches over seasons, plus some pressure (drought, population) that makes the effort worth it.
- **Writing** needs surplus → specialists → someone keeping track of who owes what.

The player doesn't click "Research Bronze." They look at a society and wonder why it hasn't figured out metallurgy yet, and the answer is that there's no tin within 400km.

## Knowledge lives in people

This is the best anthro idea to steal. Technology isn't stored in a global progress bar. It lives in individuals and gets passed on by teaching and imitation. Two consequences:

- **You can lose tech.** If the only master flint-knapper dies in a flood, the skill can disappear. This really happened: Tasmania was cut off from mainland Australia and gradually lost several technologies.
- **Population and connectedness drive innovation**, not "science points." Small isolated groups stagnate and trading networks flourish. Joseph Henrich calls this the "collective brain."

## Domestication follows real rules

Not every animal can be domesticated. The animal needs a flexible diet, fast breeding, a calm temperament, and a herd hierarchy people can take over. Jared Diamond's point that zebras aren't horses, and that this shaped history, could be a mechanic. You can give a region wolves, but whether they become dogs depends on what the people do with them over generations.

## What are you? My pick: a god who's *misunderstood*

You can only act on the environment: weather, rivers, planting species, nudging animal traits, maybe a disease. You can't give orders. The societies watch what happens and **build theories about you**.

Say you send rain the day after they burned a goat. They notice the pattern and it turns into a ritual. Now they burn goats every drought, whether you respond or not. Their religion is their model of you, and it can be right, wrong, or hilariously superstitious. Religion then shapes behavior (food taboos, who holds power, whether they migrate), so your "gifts" have downstream effects you didn't intend.

Black & White (2001) touched on this, but only with one creature, not whole cultures.

## Where ML fits naturally (no forcing)

1. **Superstition is literally learning.** People noticing links between their actions and outcomes, including false ones, is just a learning algorithm. Each culture could run a small learner that tries to predict what the "god" does. Skinner's pigeons developed rituals exactly this way. This is the strongest fit, because the ML *is* the game mechanic.
2. **Cultural transmission as social learning.** Agents copy successful or high-status neighbors, with some error. That's a simple, well-studied model, and it produces fashions, traditions, and drift for free.
3. **Evolution.** Genetic algorithms on creature traits (and maybe the people's) are the obvious fit if you play with evolution.
4. **Emergent language (stretch goal).** There's research where agents invent their own words to coordinate. Neighboring tribes could end up with dialects that drift apart.
5. **LLMs: only for flavor.** They could write a chronicle or a myth text from the sim's event log. Never use them to run the simulation itself, or it becomes unpredictable and expensive.

## Games worth looking at

WorldBox (god sandbox), Dwarf Fortress's legends mode (emergent history), Creatures (neural-net creatures, 1996), Ancestors: The Humankind Odyssey, Species: ALRE (evolution sim), and RimWorld (a storyteller AI that paces events).

## How to start small

Don't build a world. Build **one valley, one band of ~30 people, one generation of time**. Give them fire, a few plants, one animal, and a drought. Get "they figured out food storage because winters were harsh and there was clay by the river" to emerge on its own. If that moment feels magical, you've got a game. Everything else scales out from there.

## Questions that change the design

- **Time scale:** do you watch individuals (RimWorld-close) or generations flashing by (WorldBox-far)? Maybe it zooms between the two?
- **One society or many?** Contact, trade, and conquest between cultures you shaped differently could be the endgame.
- **Is there a goal?** Or is it a sandbox where the "score" is the story your world tells?

If you want, I can write this up as a proper design doc or build a tiny playable prototype of the one-valley sim. The superstition-learning mechanic would be a fun thing to test first.

### Claude (Assistant)

You're right on both counts. Isaac isn't the tool for this, and the people should be abstract enough that their hands never "do" anything for real. Here's how the pieces fit.

## Why not Isaac or RL for the bodies

Isaac Lab trains physical motor control: balancing, walking, grasping with simulated joints. It's great for robots, and it's also why people in those demos look like wobbly ragdolls. For this game it would be:

- **Wrong layer:** the interesting question is *why* someone decided to knap flint, not *how* their fingers move.
- **Too expensive:** hundreds of agents living through generations at 100x speed can't each run a physics policy.
- **Uncontrollable:** you'd spend months getting someone to hold a rock.

So the body is **puppeted** and the mind is **simulated**. This is how The Sims, RimWorld, and Dwarf Fortress all work.

## Two layers, strictly separated

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SIMULATION (plain C#, no Unity dependency) │
│  agents, needs, knowledge, beliefs, world   │
│  ticks at any speed, can run headless       │
└──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                   │ events: "Asha starts KNAP at rock#3, 40 min"
┌──────────────────▼──────────────────────────┐
│  PRESENTATION (Unity)                       │
│  picks animation, walks her there, plays    │
│  loop, spawns flakes particle, done         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The sim never knows animations exist. That means you can:

- Run 500 years headless in seconds to test whether agriculture ever emerges.
- Fast-forward in-game, where the presentation just skips or interpolates.
- Swap the art style later without touching the game logic.

## What an "agent" actually is

Each person is a bundle of data plus a decision loop:

- **Body:** age, health, hunger, fatigue, and simple heritable traits (strength, cold tolerance).
- **Personality:** a few sliders like curiosity, sociability, caution, and status-seeking.
- **Knowledge:** skills with a level, e.g. `knapping: 0.4`, `fire: 0.9`, `storage: 0.0`. Knowledge is per person, so if they die, it's gone.
- **Beliefs:** learned expectations like "gathering at the river in dry season → usually good" and "burning a goat → rain?".
- **Relationships:** kin, friends, and who they learn from.
- **Memory:** a short log of notable events, which also feeds the chronicle/myths later.

**Decisions use utility AI, not RL.** Every few in-game minutes, the agent scores the available **verbs** (gather, hunt, knap, cook, rest, socialize, teach, experiment, pray, migrate). Each score is roughly how badly they need it × how well they expect it to go × their personality. They pick one, probably with some randomness so they aren't robots. The Sims has run on essentially this model for 25 years, because it's cheap, readable, and debuggable.

## Where the ML actually sits

It sits in the **expectations**, not the bodies:

- **Each agent learns from experience.** After every action, they update what they expect it to produce in this context. That's a contextual bandit, basically a tiny Q-table per person. It's cheap and it's real learning.
- **Agents learn from each other.** They copy the expectations and skills of successful or high-status people, with copying errors. That's how culture, tradition, and superstition spread and drift.
- **The god-belief is the same machinery.** Your interventions are just another thing they try to predict.

This gives you non-scripted behavior at almost no compute cost. Unity ML-Agents *could* come in later for something narrow, like animal fleeing/herding behavior, but it's optional.

## Animation: a lookup, not ML

You don't need an animation-selector model. Each verb maps to a small set of clips:

| Verb | Clips |
|---|---|
| KNAP | sit + strike loop (3 variants) |
| GATHER | walk → crouch → pick loop → stand |
| SOCIALIZE | face partner, gesture/laugh loops |
| TEACH | same as the skill, plus a watcher playing "observe" |

A Unity Animator state machine or a simple C# script picks a clip from the verb, with a random variant. Hands only need to *look* right: IK snaps a prop to the hand bone, and a particle puff sells the flint strike. Mixamo or low-poly stylized characters are plenty.

## How it avoids feeling static

Static comes from **behavior** repeating, not from animation quality. What keeps this sim alive:

1. **Needs collide.** A hungry person who's also exhausted and grieving makes an odd choice, and you notice.
2. **Knowledge is uneven.** Only three people can make fire well, so everyone gathers around them. Social structure shows up on screen.
3. **Personality spreads outcomes.** Curious people waste time experimenting, and occasionally that's how pottery gets invented.
4. **The world changes:** seasons, herds migrating, a drought year.
5. **Cheap procedural polish:** agents look at whoever's talking, idles vary, walking paths spread out, kids follow parents. These are small touches, but they do most of the "alive" work.

## Level of detail for later

When you eventually zoom out to many societies, distant groups switch from individual agents to **aggregate stats** (population, skills distribution, beliefs). They switch back to individuals when you zoom in. Totally skippable for the one-valley prototype.

## One tick, concretely

> **Day 212, dry season.** Asha (curiosity 0.8, knapping 0.4) is hungry but the river roots came up empty the last two days, so her expected value for GATHER dropped. HUNT is low because she's never hunted. She scores EXPERIMENT high and picks it. She tries mixing river clay with ash near the fire and gets a 4% insight roll, which fails. Meanwhile Tomo watches her. He copies nothing because she didn't succeed, but her status drops slightly.
>
> **Presentation:** Asha walks to the fire, crouch-kneads a clay loop, and her thought bubble shows a question mark.

## Suggested path

1. **Headless sim first, maybe 1–2 weeks.** One valley on a grid, ~30 agents, 6–8 verbs, seasons, 3 discoverable techs (fire mastery, storage, pottery). Output a log plus charts. The only question it answers is whether emergence ever feels surprising.
2. **Unity with capsules.** Hook it to the event stream using colored capsules and floating icons. This is where you tune whether watching it is fun.
3. **Real art and animation** only after step 2 is fun.

For step 1 I could build a quick version right now, either a browser demo you can watch or a plain C# library you'd drop straight into Unity later. The C# option wastes less work since Unity is the target.

### Claude (Assistant)

### Claude (Assistant)

Barely. Either machine is way more than this needs.

**Why it doesn't matter much:**
- **The simulation runs on the CPU.** Needs, utility scoring, and the little learning tables are just math on small numbers. A few hundred agents is trivial for any modern CPU, and even thousands is fine with a bit of care.
- **The "ML" I described is tiny.** Bandit tables and social copying need no GPU, no CUDA, and no training runs. Each agent learns during play.
- **Rendering low-poly people in a valley** is light work. Integrated graphics could probably handle the prototype.

**Where the GPU *would* matter later (all optional):**
- **A local LLM for myths/chronicles.** The 5080's 16GB of VRAM can comfortably run a ~8–14B model quantized. The 5070 laptop (8GB) can run smaller ~7–8B models. This is the one place the 5080 is clearly better.
- **Unity ML-Agents training**, if you ever train animal behaviors. Both cards handle it, and honestly most ML-Agents training is CPU-bound anyway.
- **Heavier visuals** if the art style gets ambitious (lots of foliage, lighting, crowds).

**Practical suggestion:** develop on whichever you like, and use the **laptop as your "minimum spec" test machine**. If it runs well there, it'll run well for most players.

What will matter for the prototype: a decent CPU (you almost certainly have one) and enough RAM to run Unity comfortably, so 16GB minimum and 32GB is nicer.

Want me to start on the headless C# sim? I'd set it up as a plain .NET project in this folder that you can run from the terminal now and drop into Unity later. That needs the .NET SDK installed. I can check whether you have it.

### Claude (Assistant)

You don't need Unity yet. Keep it installed and ready, but the first milestone works better without it.

I checked your PC:
- **Unity 6.3** (6000.3.9f1) is installed, which is good for later.
- **Git** is installed.
- **The .NET SDK is missing.** That's the one thing you need to install.

## Why skip Unity for now

The first question is whether the sim produces surprising emergence, and answering it means running hundreds of years in seconds, rerunning with different seeds, and reading logs and charts. Unity slows that loop down with domain reloads, play mode, and scene setup. A terminal app runs in about a second.

The trick is to write the sim **so that Unity can use it later unchanged**.

## The stack

| Thing | Choice | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Language | **C#** | Same language as Unity, so nothing gets rewritten |
| Runtime | **.NET SDK 8 or 10** | Builds and runs the sim from the terminal |
| Core library target | **netstandard2.1** | The format Unity 6 can load directly |
| Editor | **VS Code + C# Dev Kit**, or **JetBrains Rider** (free for non-commercial use) | Rider is excellent with Unity later |
| Version control | **Git** (already installed) | Commit early, since experiments will break things |
| Output | **CSV logs + a simple HTML chart viewer** | Lets you see population, skills, and beliefs over time |

## Project layout

```
Ideas and Thoughts/
└── Anthro/
    ├── Anthro.Sim/        ← core library (netstandard2.1): agents, world, verbs, learning
    │                         NO Unity references, ever
    ├── Anthro.Runner/     ← console app: "run 300 years, seed 42", writes logs/CSVs
    └── Anthro.Tests/      ← a few sanity tests (agents eat, people die, knowledge transfers)
```

Later, `Anthro.Sim` gets dropped into a Unity project (as a DLL or source folder), and Unity becomes just the viewer listening to sim events.

## What I'd build first, in order

1. **World:** a small grid valley with a river, plant patches that regrow by season, a clay deposit, flint, and a herd of one animal.
2. **Agents:** needs (hunger, fatigue, social), personality, skills, age and death, birth, and kinship.
3. **Verbs + utility AI:** gather, hunt, rest, socialize, knap, teach, experiment.
4. **Learning:** per-agent expected outcomes, plus copying from successful people.
5. **One emergent discovery chain:** fire mastery → storage → pottery, gated by real conditions.
6. **Runner output:** a readable chronicle ("Year 34: Asha's grandson is the last who can make pots") plus CSVs.

## Your one step

Install the .NET SDK. I can run this for you if you want:

```bash
winget install Microsoft.DotNet.SDK.8
```

Once it's installed, say go and I'll scaffold `Anthro/` and start on steps 1–3.

[PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md) has two lists. **Decided** holds only things you've explicitly agreed to, and right now that's just two lines (sim separate from Unity, start small). **Open** is everything else. Ideas I pitched stay Open until you confirm them.

## Things I filled in that you haven't agreed to

Here's what I'd put on the table so you can see what I assumed:

- The player is a god who **only acts through the environment** and can't give orders
- People form **religion by trying to predict you**
- Tech is **lost when the people who know it die**
- The real-world **domestication rules** (Diamond's criteria)
- People as utility-AI agents, using **verbs** like gather/knap/teach
- Prehistoric setting, starting with ~30 people in one valley
- No win condition / sandbox
- ML limited to small learning tables, with LLMs only for flavor

## How I'd run the planning session

We'd go in this order, because each topic shapes the ones after it:

1. **The fantasy:** what it should *feel* like to play, in one or two sentences, and what moment makes someone tell a friend about it.
2. **Your role:** god or not, what powers you have, and what your limits are.
3. **Scope of time and scale:** where it starts, how far it can go (stone age → bronze age? → modern?), one society or many.
4. **The core loop:** what you actually do minute to minute, and what you're watching for.
5. **Goals:** sandbox, scenarios, or a win/lose state.
6. **Systems:** evolution, domestication, knowledge, belief, conflict, trade. Which are core and which are later.
7. **Look & feel:** camera, art style, how much you see individuals.
8. **ML:** where it earns its place, now that the rest is defined.

After each topic I'll write what we agreed into PLANNING.md and show you, so nothing slips in silently.

## Topic 1: the fantasy

Answer loosely. Rambling is fine.

1. Imagine someone's played for an hour and loves it. **What happened in that hour** that they'd describe to a friend?
2. Should it feel more like **tending a garden** (calm, watching things grow), **a puzzle** (I want X to happen, how do I set it up?), or **a drama** (tragedies, rivalries, stories)? Or a mix, and in what proportion?
3. **How much should players care about individual people?** Like named characters with stories, or more like watching an ant colony?
4. Is there any game, show, book, or documentary that's **closest to the vibe** you're imagining, even partly?

I updated [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md) with everything you said under **Decided**, worded as closely to yours as I could. Read it over and tell me if I misheard anything.

There are four tensions in your vision. They're the most important calls in the whole design, because they shape everything else, so I'm putting them first. For each one I give my suggestion, labeled as such, and you decide.

## 1. Destiny vs. pathways

Your founding question is whether people naturally discover certain things. Hidden tech pathways and an arc toward modernity partly answer that in advance.

- **Convergent:** everyone eventually gets to bronze → iron → steam. It differs only in speed and order.
- **Contingent:** some cultures never get metallurgy. They might go deep on astronomy, or plant medicine, or seafaring instead. "Modern" could look very different across islands.

*My suggestion:* **many paths up the same mountain, with a few dead ends.** There are hidden pathways, but several routes to each milestone, and some cultures may plateau for good unless contact or the god breaks the deadlock. That keeps your "does destiny exist?" question open *in play*, and players will argue about it.

## 2. How speculative is the biology?

Domestication and language can be very grounded. Amphibious and gliding humans are "what if" territory.

*My suggestion:* **grounded rules, speculative outcomes.** Traits only evolve under real selection pressure over real-ish time. So island people who dive for food over thousands of years could get webbed hands, better breath-holding, and seal-like fat. Each trait pathway is plausible on its own, even if the end result looks alien. The authenticity lives in the *process*.

## 3. Individuals vs. 50,000 years (the big one)

You want to know named people *and* reach the steam engine. A human life is about 40 years, and the stone age alone is tens of thousands of years.

*Options:*
- **Time flows at variable speed:** you fast-forward through quiet centuries and the game slows down for key moments (a discovery is close, a prophet is born, a disaster hits). People become a blur and then come into focus again.
- **Eras as chapters:** each era plays at human scale, then time jumps hundreds or thousands of years, with a summary of what happened in between.
- **Generational focus:** you follow a lineage, so your "favorite person" becomes their great-great-grandchildren.

*My suggestion:* **variable speed plus lineages.** It fits your "I'd been watching this guy" moments and the garden/puzzle/drama mix.

## 4. Does the player know the pathways?

No Man's Sky's discovery feel implies the *player* figures things out too.

*My suggestion:* **the player discovers them alongside the people.** You start knowing nothing about what leads to pottery. You learn by watching, experimenting with nudges, and reading hints. A codex fills in as you learn. The fun is that players actually learn real anthropology by doing it.

---

Once these are settled, the next batch is the god's powers and limits, prophets, pre-birth shaping, and what happens on extinction. Take any of these four in any order, and push back hard on my suggestions if they don't feel right.

I logged all four as decided in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md), plus the old-god idea as a *leaning*, not final.

You're right that this doesn't need heavy ML. The "scatter of event tiles" can be fully authored and still produce countless paths. Here's how I picture one tile working, so we can make sure we mean the same thing, since this is the backbone of the game.

## What a discovery tile looks like

**Example: Bow and arrow**

| Condition type | Requirement | Ways to satisfy it |
|---|---|---|
| Material | springy wood | yew, ash, *or* bamboo, *or* horn + sinew composite |
| Material | string | sinew (needs hunting), *or* plant fiber (needs cordage) |
| Knowledge | cordage | its own tile |
| Knowledge | projectile hunting | spear-throwing *or* atlatl |
| Pressure | a reason | fast/wary prey, *or* conflict with neighbors |
| Person | the right individual | someone curious who's skilled at hunting *or* woodwork, and who happens to be in the right situation |

When every row is satisfied, the discovery **becomes possible, not guaranteed**. It gets a chance each time a qualifying person does a related activity. Then it has to **spread** through teaching, or it dies with them.

Here's why this produces countless paths:
- **OR-options** mean different cultures arrive by different routes. A bamboo-and-plant-fiber bow culture really does differ from a horn-and-sinew one, and that flavor can carry forward.
- **Tiles feed tiles**, so the "scatter" is really a big web where some tiles are reachable from many directions.
- **Pressures** mean a comfortable culture may *never* bother, even with all the materials. That's your dead ends and your Pandora-style divergence.
- **Your role as god** is to push missing rows into place: plant bamboo, send fast antelope, nudge a curious kid's birth. Then you hope.

Does that match what you picture? This one is worth a firm yes or a correction.

## Tension: what counts as "advanced"?

If a Pandora-like culture and an industrial one are both "successful," the game needs to recognize that somehow, even without a win screen. A few options:

- **Dimensions instead of a score:** for example *mastery of nature, knowledge, wellbeing, reach (how far they travel/influence), complexity (social/technical), and continuity (how long the culture's lasted)*. Cultures come out as different shapes, like a radar chart.
- **Milestones of a different kind:** "first to sail to another island," "first written record," "a city of 10,000," "lived 1,000 years without famine." These can be reached in many ways.
- **No judgment at all:** the game just describes cultures, and you decide what you're proud of.

*My suggestion:* dimensions + milestones. That honors many kinds of success without crowning a winner. What do you think?

## Next batch: the god's hands

1. **Powers:** what do you imagine being able to do? Your list so far is placing animals, natural features, resources, nudges, prophets, and pre-birth shaping. Anything bigger (earthquakes, plagues, climate shifts), or deliberately not?
2. **Limits:** should powers be **free**, or cost something? Some options:
   - a **faith/belief currency**: the more people believe in you, the more you can do, which ties into the misinterpretation mechanic
   - **cooldowns**
   - **natural consequences only**: you can do anything, but the ecosystem reacts, e.g. drop in wolves and they eat your deer
3. **Prophet:** how do you picture talking to them? Picking from a few messages? Typing freely (this is where an LLM could really fit)? And do they get your message right, or garble it?
4. **Pre-birth shaping:** how often does it come up, and what can you shape? Personality, talents, physical traits?
5. **Extinction:** if everyone dies, do you restart in the same world, which keeps your changes to animals and land, or start fresh? Is there a way to lose the whole game?

All logged in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md). Your answers raised two technical tensions, both about where ML fits, so they're worth settling before anything else.

## Tension 1: the mind map, learned vs. legible

Your brain-map idea is the right place for ML, and it's a natural fit. The catch is that a deep neural net is a black box. You couldn't draw it as a readable mind map, you couldn't say why someone did something, and pre-birth shaping would have nothing clear to grab onto.

*My suggestion:* **the mind is a learned graph.**
- **Nodes** are readable things: `fire`, `river`, `the god`, `wolves`, `Asha`, `storing food`, `curiosity`.
- **Links** carry learned weights: "fire → warmth +0.9", "burning goats → rain +0.4 (wrong, but believed)", "Asha → trustworthy +0.7".
- **Learning** happens from experience (things that happen together get linked), from being taught, and from copying others. This is real, well-studied learning. It's just not deep learning.
- **Choices** come from scoring activities using the graph plus needs and personality.
- **The visual you wanted** falls straight out of this: a glowing web of a person's mind, with strong beliefs bright and fading ones dim. Current thoughts are whichever nodes are lit up right now.
- **Shaping** becomes concrete: pre-birth, you set trait nodes. At a branch point, you strengthen one link.

It's cheap enough to run for thousands of people, and every behavior is explainable. If we later want a neural piece for something narrow, it can plug in, but I wouldn't build the core on one.

## Tension 2: the free-typed prophet

Free typing works well if the LLM is only a **translator**. You type "tell them to stop burning the forest and plant seeds by the river." The LLM turns that into structured commands the sim understands (`TEACH: planting @ river`, `FORBID: burning @ forest`) and writes the prophet's sermon text. The sim never depends on the LLM's judgment.

The open questions are practical:
- **Cloud API:** best quality, but costs money per use and needs internet.
- **Local model:** free and offline. Your 5080 handles it fine, but weaker PCs struggle.
- **Fallback:** contextual menu options always exist, so it works without an LLM.

*My suggestion:* design it menu-first, with free typing layered on top. Decide cloud vs. local much later.

## Your open questions

**Pre-birth frequency:** tie it to belief, so it's a limited power. For example, a few "blessed births" per generation, more if belief is strong. Prophet lineages and devout families might offer them more often. That makes it scarce and meaningful, which fits "I engineered this kid and waited 20 years."

**Natural consequences, keeping it buildable:**
- **In:** predator-prey populations, plant regrowth, overhunting, soil exhaustion, disease spreading through dense settlements. All of these can be built.
- **Out (or faked):** real climate or fluid simulation. Big climate events are scripted effects with simple rules.

## Next batch: the core loop and the world

These matter most for what we build first.

1. **What does a typical 10 minutes of play look like?** For example: watch the camp, notice people are cold and hungry in winter, open the codex, see a hint about storing food, place clay by the river, wait, a kid starts playing with the clay, a notification says they're at a branch point, you push them... Describe it however you see it.
2. **World:** a real Earth-like map, or **invented** islands/continents generated per game? Procedural generation means every game is different.
3. **Starting point:** do you start with already-modern-ish humans (*Homo sapiens* bands), or earlier hominins that evolution can take in odd directions?
4. **Priority:** now that you've seen how things fit, which 2–3 systems feel most essential to prove the game is fun? My guess is **people's minds + discovery tiles + god nudges**, with evolution, language, and multi-society later. But this is your call.

### Claude (Assistant)

First, how traits and interests drive decisions, since that's the heart of the people system. Then the visuals, then a full relay of everything with the gaps I found.

## 1. How traits, interests, and lessons all shape decisions

Learned lessons are just one layer. A person's mind has several layers that change at different speeds:

| Layer | Examples | Changes | Where it comes from |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Traits** | curiosity, caution, sociability, patience, aggression, dexterity | almost never | genes + pre-birth shaping |
| **Interests** | stone, animals, plants, sky, spirits, people, water | slowly, over years | enjoyment, mentors, childhood experiences |
| **Skills** | knapping 0.6, tracking 0.2 | through practice | doing and being taught |
| **Beliefs / lessons** | "river roots fail in dry season," "the god likes fire" | constantly | experience, teaching, copying others |
| **Relationships** | trusts Asha, fears Tomo | constantly | interactions |
| **State** | hungry, grieving, afraid, inspired | minute to minute | body + events |
| **Culture** (shared) | taboos, customs, what's "proper" | across generations | the group's shared beliefs |

**When choosing an activity, each layer pulls differently:**

```
score = need it solves         (hunger makes FORAGE urgent)
      × expected result        (beliefs: "foraging at the river works")
      + interest bonus         (she likes plants, so it's rewarding in itself)
      + trait pulls            (curious → EXPERIMENT, cautious → avoids HUNT)
      + social pull            (friends are going; elder told her to)
      − culture                (taboo: nobody forages the burial grove)
      + a bit of randomness
```

**Traits also change how people learn**, which is where it gets interesting:
- **Cautious** people weight bad outcomes heavily, so one wolf attack makes them avoid the forest for years.
- **Stubborn** people barely update beliefs. They keep old superstitions longer, which is great for tradition and bad for progress.
- **Sociable** people copy others more. **Curious** people try untested things.
- **Interests grow from enjoyment.** A kid who loves stone and has a patient knapper grandfather becomes the specialist, and specialists are who trigger discovery tiles.

**Example: same situation, different people.** Winter, food is low.
- **Asha** (curious, likes plants) experiments with drying berries by the fire. → She could trigger *food preservation*.
- **Tomo** (bold, likes animals, good tracker) goes on a risky hunt.
- **Elder Ren** (cautious, stubborn, devout) organizes a ritual, because "last time we burned a goat, the snow stopped."

All three are reasonable choices from their minds, and the camp suddenly feels like real people. Does this layering match what you imagined?

## 2. Visuals and perspective (not decided, here are the trade-offs)

| Option | Feel | Cost / risk |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Full 3D free camera** (Sims, Planet Zoo) | most immersive close-ups | highest: animation, terrain, and camera all have to look good from every angle, which is where jank shows |
| **B. Stylized 3D, guided camera** (Timberborn, Against the Storm, Townscaper) | tilted view, rotate/zoom, little readable people, can still zoom in close | moderate: stylization hides simple animation, and the camera limits avoid ugly angles |
| **C. Hades-style painted pseudo-3D** | gorgeous | very high art cost: every body shape and animation hand-drawn. **Evolution makes this explode**, because webbed or hairy or gliding people all need redrawn art |
| **D. 2D top-down** (RimWorld) | cheapest | loses the "see little people up close" feel you want |

**The deciding factor is evolution.** Changing bodies are far easier in 3D: swap parts, morph shapes, change hair/skin via shaders. That points strongly to 3D. *My suggestion:* **B, stylized low-poly 3D with a guided camera**, plus a close-up "inspect" mode. Zooming out turns into a **stylized map view** (like a painted chart) instead of a seamless globe, which is far easier to build. Look up **Timberborn** in particular: management-scale, stylized 3D, little characters, and it looks great without complex animation.

## 3. Full relay

I rewrote [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md) as one organized design doc so it's all in one place. Here are the parts I most want you to check: things we haven't talked about at all.

### Gaps we haven't touched (section 15)

The first two matter most for the build:

1. **Who's building this, and how long?** Solo, a team, a hobby, or a commercial release? This sets how big the first version can realistically be, and it's the biggest unknown left.
2. **Settlements & building:** do people build huts and villages themselves? Can you ever place man-made things, or only natural ones?
3. **Conflict & war** between groups: in scope? How dark?
4. **Family, gender roles, reproduction, aging:** fully emergent, or modeled with defaults?
5. **Tone & sensitivity:** the project is inspired by real Indigenous history and colonization. With invented cultures we avoid appropriation, but we need a stance on things like sacrifice, slavery, and conquest. Are they in the game, and how are they framed?
6. Smaller items for later: trade, disease, UI, sound, platform, the name.

### Priority (section 16)

When you lay the systems out, the order mostly picks itself, because each system needs the ones before it. Belief needs minds *and* god powers, evolution needs generations, and so on. Here's the proposed first milestone:

> One band of ~30 in a small generated valley, simple ecology, the full mind model, ~10 discovery tiles (fire → cordage → storage → pottery → bow), 4–5 god powers, and belief forming from your actions.

That proves the three things the whole game rests on: minds feel alive, convergence-based discovery is fun, and being misinterpreted is fun. Evolution, language, and multiple societies come after, and none of them work without those three anyway.

**To answer:** does the mind layering match what you imagined, does option B work for visuals, and what are your thoughts on gaps 1–5? Skim the doc for anything I got wrong too.

Logged in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md). The mind model, option B, the Planet Zoo 2-style click-to-zoom view, the solo + AI setup, natural-only creation, and your stance on conflict and dark content are all **Decided** now. The first milestone is locked too.

## 4. Family, reproduction, gender, aging, elaborated

These decide how a society *reproduces itself*, both biologically and culturally. My suggestion is to split them by what's fixed and what varies:

**Fixed biology (hard rules):**
- **Pregnancy, birth, infancy.** Long childhoods matter: kids need years of care, and that's when they absorb skills and beliefs. A culture that loses its elders loses its teachers.
- **Aging:** strength fades, but knowledge and status can grow. Elders become knowledge keepers, which ties into tech being lost when people die.
- **Fertility and mortality** respond to food, disease, and danger. Population growth comes out of the sim, not a slider.
- **Inheritance of traits:** kids blend their parents' traits with some mutation. This is what evolution runs on.

**Cultural variables (emerge as customs, different per society):**
- **Pairing:** monogamy, polygamy, arranged matches, loose bonds.
- **Kinship:** who counts as family, and whether lineage passes through the mother or the father.
- **Division of labor:** whether roles split by sex, by age, by skill, or not at all. This is **not hardcoded**. Real societies varied a lot, so it should emerge from pressures and customs.
- **Who raises children:** parents, grandparents, the whole band.

Why this split matters for gameplay: cultural variables shape **who learns what from whom**. That decides which skills survive and which discoveries happen. A culture where only men hunt and only women gather passes knowledge differently than one where everyone does everything. That's real anthropology turning into game mechanics.

Is that split right? Anything you'd make fixed that I left cultural, or the reverse?

## What our people look like

Good call to establish this before anything else visual. Two clarifications:
- **Townscaper** is buildings only, but its look (soft shapes, clean colors, cozy) can absolutely set the world style.
- **The Sims** gets natural-looking people from a huge animation library and a big team. As a solo dev, we can get that same *feel* with fewer animations by using a stylized body that forgives simplicity.

Questions to shape the character design:
1. **Proportions:** chunky and cute (big head, ~3–4 heads tall), stylized but human (~5–6 heads, like Sims 4 or Fortnite), or more realistic? Chunkier is cheaper and hides animation shortcuts. More human makes evolution changes (webbed hands, fur, gliding membranes) easier to read.
2. **Faces:** expressive faces, which helps a lot with Fallout Shelter-style personality, or simple/featureless?
3. **Mood words:** pick 3. For example: *warm, earthy, cozy, mysterious, vibrant, painterly, toy-like, somber, mythic, handcrafted.*

If you want, I can make a quick visual page with a few style directions side by side to react to. That's easier than describing them in words.

## 6. Leave it for later

Trade, disease, UI, sound, platform, and the name are logged in section 16. None of them change the first milestone, so we can skip them for now.

## What's left before building

1. Your answer on family/culture above.
2. A character look direction. It isn't needed for the headless sim, so it can run in parallel.
3. **Spec the first milestone in detail:** the ~10 discovery tiles drawn as an actual web with conditions, the exact mind model fields, the verbs, and the 4–5 god powers. That's the last planning doc before code.

Your Synty library mostly settles the visuals. Everything should match the **Synty POLYGON** style you already own, and human animation is a solved problem for this kind of game. Logged as proposed in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md).

## The key technical fact: Unity's Humanoid rig

Unity can apply **any humanoid animation to any humanoid character**. So we're not locked into one vendor: Mixamo, Synty, and other Asset Store packs all mix together. That's what gives you the big, varied set of animations you were hoping for.

## People: Synty Sidekick

Synty's [Sidekick modular characters](https://syntystore.com/blogs/blog/introducing-sidekick-character-creator) look like the best fit:
- Built from 1,400+ parts, so every person can look different.
- Body blend shapes cover masculine/feminine, slim/heavy, and muscular, and it can switch species. That's great for **evolution** and for inherited looks.
- Facial blend shapes for expressions, which helps the Fallout Shelter-style personality.
- Uses the Unity Humanoid rig, so every animation pack below works with it.
- It already matches your Synty nature packs.

## Animation candidates

| Source | What it covers | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **Mixamo** | Huge general library: walking, sitting, praying, dancing, carrying, talking | Free |
| **[Synty ANIMATION – Idles](https://assetstore.unity.com/packages/3d/animations/synty-animation-idles-299700)** + [Base Locomotion](https://syntystore.com/products/animation-base-locomotion) | 330 idles plus movement, matched to Synty characters. Idles are what make people feel alive | ~$50 each |
| **[Kevin Iglesias – Villager Animations](https://assetstore.unity.com/packages/3d/animations/villager-animations-147875)** | 72 gathering and work animations: farming, fishing, hammering, mining, gathering. Exactly our verbs | ~$28 (there's a [free version](https://kevdev.itch.io/human-villager-animations-free) to try first) |

Specific crafts like knapping flint or shaping pots won't have exact animations. We cover those with a generic work loop (crouched hammering, kneading) plus a prop in the hand plus a particle effect, which sells it at Planet Zoo scale.

## Two things to watch

1. **The "stock Synty" look.** Synty is very recognizable. We can make it ours with a custom color palette, lighting, and post-processing. That's a later polish step.
2. **Check before buying:** do Sidekick characters work as **children and elders**? Do parts exist for **fur, webbing, and primitive clothing**? Children matter a lot for this game, since that's when learning happens.

**Don't buy anything yet.** Character visuals aren't needed until the Unity phase. You can try Sidekick's free starter pack and Kevin Iglesias's free animations whenever you're curious.

Next is speccing the first milestone (the ~10-tile web, mind fields, verbs, god powers). Say when you're ready.

- [Human Villager Animations FREE – itch.io](https://kevdev.itch.io/human-villager-animations-free)

### Claude (Assistant)

Yes, eventually. But buildings in this game are trickier than in most, so how we handle them matters more than which pack you buy.

## Why one building pack won't cover it

1. **The range is huge:** from windbreaks and hide tents to stone temples, windmills, and steam-era factories.
2. **Cultures should look different.** A bamboo-and-thatch culture and a stone-and-hide culture shouldn't share a house model. Pandora-style cultures need their own architecture.
3. **People build, you don't.** Buildings come out of the sim (materials + knowledge + need), so they need **construction stages**, not just finished models.

## Proposed approach: building styles come from materials

Same idea as the discovery tiles. A building is a **type** (shelter, storage, workshop, shrine...) plus the **materials** that culture has available:

| | Wood + hide | Bamboo + thatch | Stone + mud |
|---|---|---|---|
| Shelter | hide tent | stilt hut | stone roundhouse |
| Storage | raised cache | woven granary | clay-lined pit |
| Shrine | carved post | flower altar | standing stones |

So architecture really does differ by culture, for the same reason the bows do. In assets, that means **modular kits** (walls, roofs, frames, supports) in several materials that we combine, rather than many unique buildings.

## For the first milestone: probably nothing to buy

Early structures are simple: fire pit, windbreak, lean-to, hide tent, drying rack, storage pit, maybe a kiln. Most of that can be **kitbashed from your nature packs** (logs, branches, rocks, leaves) plus a few hide and clay props. That's arguably more authentic, since early buildings literally were piles of nature.

## Later candidates to look at

- **Synty packs that match your style:** [Fantasy Village](https://syntystore.com/products/polygon-fantasy-village) (huts, villages, props), [Ancient Egypt](https://syntystore.com/products/polygon-ancient-egypt) (monumental stone, temples), plus Viking or Ancient Empire for wood halls and classical stone.
- **A Stone Age pack:** Synty doesn't seem to have a true prehistoric set. There's one called ["EXPLORER – Stone Age"](https://gfx-hub.co/unity-asset/3d-model/77726-explorer-stone-age.html) with huts, tents, megaliths, and shrines, but I couldn't confirm who makes it or whether it matches Synty's style. Find the official Asset Store page and check before buying.

I've logged the materials-driven building idea as **proposed** in the doc.

Two corrections first, then the licensing catch, which matters more than the price.

**Price:** it's **$40/mo with a 3-month prepaid minimum, so $120 upfront**, not under $90. You do get $10/month of store credit back.

## The catch: it's a subscription license

I read Synty's [Standard Subscription Licence](https://syntystore.com/pages/standard-subscription-licence). After you cancel:

- ✅ A game that's **already released** can keep selling, and you can ship **minor bug fixes**.
- ❌ You **can't substantially develop, modify, or improve** it with those assets anymore.
- ❌ You can't use them in anything new, or make new marketing materials with them.

So 3 months of dev time doesn't work the way it sounds. You can't grab everything, cancel, and keep building. **You'd need to stay subscribed for the entire development period, until release.** A game like this is realistically a multi-year solo project, so that's roughly **$480/year, every year, until you ship.**

## Comparison

| | SyntyPass | Buying packs |
|---|---|---|
| Cost | $480/yr while developing | ~$200 per Sidekick pack, once |
| Owned forever | No, only while subscribed | Yes |
| Variety | Everything | Only what you buy |
| Best when | heavy asset phase, shipping within ~1–2 years | long development, a known set of packs |

## My suggestion: don't subscribe yet

- **The next several weeks are headless sim work.** We're not touching art, so a subscription would just burn money.
- When we reach the Unity visual phase, decide based on how close release is. If shipping is within ~1–2 years and you need lots of variety, SyntyPass makes sense then.
- Meanwhile, the **free Sidekick starter pack** lets you test the character creator for children, elders, and primitive looks at no cost.

**Downloading:** the licence and FAQ don't say how it works (launcher vs. direct `.unitypackage` downloads), so I won't guess. [support@syntystudios.com](mailto:support@syntystudios.com) can answer that before you buy.

**Construction:** agreed, Clash of Clans-style is the right call. A covered/scaffolded placeholder with a few progress stages until it's finished is cheap, readable, and works for every building type and material. I'll add it to the doc.

Summer changes things, in a good way for the asset budget and in a serious way for scope.

## SyntyPass: summer makes the math work

About 9 months at $40/mo is **~$360 total**, which is fair for Sidekick plus the whole Synty library. And yes, for a later substantial update you'd **resubscribe** for that window (minimum 3 months, so $120).

On "only if it's commercial": the licence I read **doesn't make a non-commercial exception** for the post-cancellation rules. I wouldn't count on one. Ask Synty support along with your download question.

**One ordering tip:** if you're subscribing anyway, check what's *in the Synty library* (animals, villages, tribal or ancient packs) **before** buying anything below. Some of it may already be covered.

## The packs

| Pack | Price now | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **Poly Art: Animal Forest Set** (Malbers) | $90 | ✅ **Strong yes.** Wolf, deer, stag, boar, bear, moose, fox, rabbit: exactly early-human prey and predators. Check whether its animations need Malbers' controller to run. |
| **Low Poly Animated Prehistoric Animals** (polyperfect) | $30 | ✅ **Yes.** Mammoth, sabertooth, and so on. Megafauna being hunted to extinction is a real, great natural-consequence story. |
| **Kevin Iglesias Human Mega Animations** | $65 | ✅ **Likely yes,** but compare with his Villager Animations ($28, 72 work animations). Make sure Mega actually includes the villager/work set before choosing. |
| **EXPLORER – Stone Age** (SICS Games) | $53 | 🟡 **Maybe.** Huts, villages, props. Check whether the style sits next to Synty. |
| **Low Poly Prehistoric Pack** (WhaleForge) | $35 | 🟡 **Maybe.** Caves, statues, shaman props. Small but on-theme. |
| **Low Poly Animated Animals** (polyperfect) | $50 | 🟡 **Maybe.** Horse, dog, and elephant matter for domestication later. Lots of overlap otherwise. |
| **Poly Universal Pack** (polyperfect) | $225 | ⏸ **Wait.** Big and covers later eras, including steampunk, but it's expensive and may overlap with SyntyPass. |
| **Animal Controller** (Malbers) | $25 | ❌ **Skip.** It's built for player-controlled creatures. Our sim moves the animals; we only need to play their animations. |
| **Horse AnimSet Pro** (riding) | — | ❌ **Skip for now.** Same issue: player riding. Horse domestication is far down the web. |
| **Low Poly Dinosaurs** | — | ❌ **Skip.** Humans and dinosaurs breaks authenticity. Maybe as fossils someday. |
| **Low Poly Fantasy Creatures** | — | ❌ **Skip for now.** Possibly useful for speculative evolution much later. |
| **Stone Age Stylized** | — | ❌ Agreed, too cartoonish. |

**Mixing risk:** Synty, polyperfect, Malbers, and SICS each have slightly different looks. The usual fix is **one shared shader and color palette** applied to everything, so it all reads as one world. That's very doable, and it's also how we avoid the "stock Synty" look.

**Timing:** a lot of these are 50% off right now, and Asset Store sales end. If you want to lock in the clear yeses (Forest Set and Prehistoric Animals), buying now is reasonable. Otherwise nothing is needed until the Unity phase.

## The bigger issue: summer and scope

So far I've been planning a long open-ended project. **9 months solo** means we need to decide **what "done by summer" means**, because the full vision (evolution, language, multiple societies sailing to each other, the arc to steam power) is far more than 9 months, even with AI.

A realistic shape:

| Months | Phase |
|---|---|
| 1–2 | Headless sim: minds, tiles, ecology, god powers, belief |
| 3–4 | Unity: world, characters, camera, click-to-inspect |
| 5–7 | Content: more tiles, prophet, lineages, story beats, codex |
| 8–9 | Polish, balance, ship |

That gets you **one island, one people, from first fire to maybe early farming/villages**, with the god, belief, prophet, and discovery systems all working. That's a real, complete, shippable game. Evolution, multiple societies, and later eras become updates.

**Questions:**
1. What happens in summer: a **release** (Steam / itch.io, maybe Early Access), a **demo**, a portfolio piece, something else?
2. Does that "one island, stone age → early villages" scope for v1 feel right, or is there a system you'd fight to get into the summer version?

I went through Synty's whole POLYGON catalog: 84 packs over 4 pages. Here's how it maps to what we need. Pack names are from the store listing; I haven't opened each pack's contents, so verify the important ones.

## What SyntyPass would cover

| Need | Synty packs |
|---|---|
| **Primitive / tribal structures** | **Goblin War Camp** (rough huts, hide tents, palisades, likely the closest fit), Adventure Pack, Explorer Kit, Frog Shrine |
| **Pandora-style organic cultures** | **Elven Realm**, Enchanted Forest |
| **Early farming & villages** | **Farm Pack**, Fantasy Village, Viking Realm, Town Pack |
| **Distinct civilizations** | Ancient Egypt, Ancient Empire (Greco-Roman), Samurai Empire, Fantasy Kingdom |
| **Sailing between islands** | **Pirate Realm / Pirate Pack** (ships) |
| **Industrial era** | Western Frontier, Construction Pack, WWI map |
| **Domestication** | **Dog Pack**, Farm Pack (probably farm animals, verify) |
| **Fantasy creatures** | Kaiju Pack, Werewolf |
| **Characters** | Sidekick; **Kids Pack** might solve the children question |
| **Effects / UI** | Particle FX Pack, Icons Pack |
| **Biomes** | You already have these |

**What's missing:** a real Stone Age / prehistoric pack. Goblin War Camp plus nature kitbashing probably covers it.

**Verdict on Poly Universal Pack:** skip it. SyntyPass covers nearly the same era range in one consistent style.

(Every pack showed as "sold out" individually, which suggests Synty may be moving them into the pass only. Another reason the subscription makes sense.)

## Animals: agreed, go polyperfect

You're right on both counts. Your Malbers set is wild animals only, and mixing two animal styles in the same scene is the clash people would notice most. **polyperfect for all animals** is cleaner:
- **Low Poly Animated Animals** ($50, half off) for domestic species and many wild ones
- **Prehistoric Animals** ($30) for megafauna
- **Dinosaurs** and **Fantasy Creatures**: agreed, I was wrong to skip these. Your framing is better: this isn't Earth's history, it's **humanity evolving in alien-ish environments**. Domestication rules applied to fantasy creatures could be one of the most fun parts of the game. Can these raptors be tamed? What if they need a herd hierarchy, like horses?

Malbers becomes a backup. One check before buying: compare polyperfect next to Synty in screenshots, since polyperfect is a bit simpler and cuter.

## Low-poly: good idea?

**Yes, for this game specifically:**
- **Solo dev:** consistent style with no art team
- **Performance:** hundreds of agents plus animals plus a big map run fine
- **Evolution:** swapping parts and colors is easy, and odd bodies look intentional, not wrong
- **Readability:** at Planet Zoo scale you can tell what people are doing at a glance

**The risk** is looking like an "asset flip," which Steam players notice and judge harshly. The fix is what I mentioned: **one shared shader, color palette, and lighting** across Synty and polyperfect, plus a strong UI identity. Synty is slightly more detailed and polyperfect slightly simpler, and a shared palette blends them.

## Timeline: you're probably right

**2 weeks for the headless MVP is realistic** for the *code* with AI help. I was padding for the part that's actually slow: **tuning**. Making emergence feel surprising but not random, and making discoveries feel earned, is iterative. But that tuning continues alongside the Unity work anyway, so it doesn't block anything.

On 9 months for a full game: I'd treat **one island as the floor and the fuller vision as the stretch**. Plan the architecture for everything (multiple societies, evolution, eras), build in order, and whatever is solid by summer ships. That way we never cut corners that block later systems.

### Claude (Assistant)

Confirmed: Synty's animations **don't** cover what we need, so Kevin Iglesias is still a buy.

**What Synty has** (all 7 animation packs): Base Locomotion, Idles, Emotes & Taunts, Sword Combat, Bow Combat, Goblin Locomotion, and Dog Pack. That's movement, standing around, reacting, and fighting.

**What it's missing:** gathering, crafting, farming, carrying, hammering, fishing. Those are most of our verbs.

**Get the Mega pack, not just Villager.** My earlier "61 animations" was wrong. The [Human Mega Animations Pack](https://assetstore.unity.com/packages/3d/animations/human-mega-animations-pack-162341) has **300+ animations** and bundles 8 of his packs:

| Included pack | What it gives us |
|---|---|
| Villager | gathering, farming, crafting, hammering, carrying: our core verbs |
| Basic Motions | general movement |
| Throwing | spears and stones, for early hunting |
| Archer | the bow and arrow discovery |
| Dance | rituals, celebrations, religion |
| Melee / Soldier | conflict and war |
| Spellcasting | could pass for shamans or prophets |

At $65 on sale versus $25 for Villager alone, Mega is the better deal for this game. With Synty's Idles, Emotes, and Locomotion (included in the pass) plus free Mixamo for gaps, people animation is covered. I've updated [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md).

### Claude (Assistant)

I went through every relevant pack's actual contents. Short version: **Synty is weak at both ends of our timeline, Stone Age and steam era, and those are exactly where these three packs are strong.** So they aren't redundant.

## What Synty actually covers

| Era / need | Best Synty option | Real fit? |
|---|---|---|
| **Stone Age** | **Goblin War Camp**: wood/hide/bone, tents, log walls, thatch, stilts, fire pits | 🟡 The only primitive kit, and some of it is goblin-specific (spikes, skulls, siege engines) |
| Caves, megaliths, pottery, stone tools, rafts | none | ❌ gap |
| Timber villages | **Viking Realm**: modular halls, docks, boats, tanning racks | ✅ |
| Pandora-style organic | **Elven Realm**: modular organic architecture, cliffs, waterfalls | ✅ |
| Villages / medieval | Fantasy Village, Adventure, Fantasy Kingdom | ✅ |
| Farming | Farm Pack, **but it's a modern farm** (tractors, pickups) | 🟡 the crops are useful |
| **Livestock** | none. Fantasy Village's "animals" are cartoon animal *people* | ❌ confirms polyperfect |
| **Steam / industrial** | only Western Frontier | ❌ gap |
| Explorer Kit | modern explorer with a revolver and a 4x4 | ❌ useless to us |

## The three packs

**EXPLORER – Stone Age ($53): yes, the most directly useful thing for our first milestone**
- 47 buildings: huts, tents, camps, shrines, towers, bridges
- 201 props: pottery, tools, weapons, boats, rafts, fossils, stone wheels
- 128 rocks, cliffs, and megaliths, plus fire and fog effects
- 8 characters with 15 animations (praying, worshipping, working)

Almost every item maps to an early discovery tile. Synty has nothing like it. ⚠️ Check its screenshots for style first: it has hand-painted skyboxes and might lean more painterly than Synty.

**WhaleForge Prehistoric ($35): yes, cheap and unique**
- **Modular caves** (46 wall pieces) plus **painted cave walls**
- Statues, pots, tools, bones, 8 animals, 3 characters
- Nobody else has caves. Caves are natural early shelter, and **cave paintings** could be a great art/religion milestone. It uses one shared material, so it's easy to recolor to our palette.

**Poly Universal ($225, half off): the hard call**
7,000+ models. Here's what matters to us:

| Useful now | Useful later | Irrelevant |
|---|---|---|
| Primeval ×105, Survival ×172 | **Steampunk ×471** (the only steam-era source we've found) | City ×786, cars, planes ×760 |
| | **Modular parts ×1,235** (walls, roofs, beams: building blocks for material-based architecture) | Holidays, racetrack, movie set, guns |
| | Fantasy ×1,248, Farm ×460, sailboats, trains | |

- **For:** it fills the steam-era gap, gives us building blocks for the material-driven architecture idea, matches the polyperfect animals' style, and half off is a real discount.
- **Against:** maybe 40% is useless to us. Its Stone Age content overlaps with Explorer. And steam is our *stretch* goal for summer.
- **My take:** if the budget isn't tight, it's worth buying at this price, mostly as insurance for the later eras. If it is tight, skip it: Asset Store sales come back, and steam is the last thing we'd build. Before deciding, polyperfect posts previews on Sketchfab, including the [Farm](https://sketchfab.com/3d-models/poly-universal-pack-farm-7c9d695a18ab4c458acdb6149f2deade) and [Steampunk](https://sketchfab.com/3d-models/poly-steampunk-pack-ecfc6bbf489a45358d8e5e9e4a29362c) sets, so you can see the style.

## Two gaps nobody covers

1. **Prehistoric clothing.** Sidekick has packs for Vikings, goblins, elves, villagers, and sci-fi, but **no tribal or primitive clothing.** Our people would start out dressed like fantasy peasants. Options: recolor the simplest villager clothing to hide tones, or model a few simple hide wraps fitted to the Sidekick body. That's doable with AI-assisted Blender work, and it's probably what we'll need.
2. **Children.** Synty's Kids Pack is 100 *modern* kids (schools, skateboards). The rig would work, but the clothing has the same problem. Kids are central to learning in our sim, so we need to check whether Sidekick handles child proportions.

## Running total if you buy everything

| Item | Cost |
|---|---|
| SyntyPass to summer | ~$360 |
| Kevin Iglesias Mega | $65 |
| polyperfect Animals + Prehistoric | $80 |
| polyperfect Dinosaurs + Fantasy Creatures | not priced yet (check the sale) |
| EXPLORER Stone Age | $53 |
| WhaleForge Prehistoric | $35 |
| Poly Universal (optional) | $225 |
| **Total** | **~$595 without Universal, ~$820 with**, plus dinos/fantasy |

All of this is logged in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md), including the two gaps.

### Claude (Assistant)

You were right on caves, boats, and Universal. Corrections first.

## Caves: you already have them

I only checked the Asset Store packs before, which was a mistake. Synty caves:
- **Alpine Mountain (you own it):** icy caves, stalactites, cliffs
- **Pirate Realm:** cave floors, walls, pillars, skull cave
- **Dungeon Pack:** caverns
- **Dwarven Dungeon Map:** cavern hallways, mushroom grotto

So **WhaleForge is no longer a must-buy.** The only unique thing left in it is painted cave walls, and we can do cave paintings as decals on Synty rock.

## Boats: covered from raft to sailing ship

| Stage | Pack | Own it? |
|---|---|---|
| Raft | Tropical Jungle | ✅ |
| Canoe | Tropical Jungle, Alpine Mountain, Swamp | ✅ |
| River barges and boats | Ancient Egypt | via SyntyPass |
| Longships ×3 | Viking Realm | via SyntyPass |
| Rowboat + 9 modular sailing ships | Pirate Realm | via SyntyPass |

That's the whole early seafaring progression, half of it already in your library.

## Poly Universal: agreed, skip

Between Ancient Empire (modular villas, markets, kilns, looms, forges, farms), Egypt, Samurai (rice fields), Viking, Elven, Fantasy Village, and Farm crops, Synty covers buildings and farming well. The only real gap is the **steam era**, and that's our stretch goal anyway. I've logged Universal as skipped.

## Primitive clothing: no ready-made answer, but workable

Synty has **no tribal or caveman character pack**. I searched specifically. Here's how to integrate what exists:

1. **Mix Sidekick packs (best bet).** Sidekick parts work across packs, so a human body can wear parts from **Goblin Fighters** (probably scrappy leather and fur, since they're built for crude goblin gear), **Viking Warriors** (furs), and **Fantasy Villagers** (simple tunics). With SyntyPass you'd have all of them. I can't see the parts themselves, so verify in the character creator.
2. **Recolor with palette swaps.** Most Synty clothing gets its color from a shared palette texture, so turning a peasant tunic into hide-brown or undyed linen is a texture swap, not modeling.
3. **Regular (non-Sidekick) POLYGON characters** like Egypt villagers or Ancient Empire farmers are mostly **one mesh with clothing baked in**. You can't move their clothes onto Sidekick bodies without Blender work (doable, but real effort). We could use them whole for specific cultures, but then they lose Sidekick's body blending and evolution.
4. **Fallback:** a handful of simple custom hide wraps fitted to the Sidekick body. That's AI-assisted Blender work, and probably 5–10 pieces would cover the Stone Age.

## Female characters

I can't see them myself, but chunky stylized female characters reading masculine is a common complaint about Synty's look. Sidekick has two tools for this: **body blend shapes** (the masculine↔feminine slider can be pushed further) and **facial blend shapes**, plus hair and face parts. Our people also inherit body shapes, so we'll be setting those values in code anyway. Test it in the free Sidekick starter pack before subscribing. If even the fully feminine setting looks off, that's worth knowing early.

## Updated buy list

| Buy | Item |
|---|---|
| ✅ | SyntyPass (when you hit the Unity phase), Kevin Iglesias Mega, polyperfect Animals + Prehistoric + Dinosaurs + Fantasy Creatures |
| 🟡 | EXPLORER Stone Age: still the most Stone Age-specific pack (huts, pottery, megaliths), but check its style next to Synty first |
| ❌ | Poly Universal, WhaleForge (optional), Malbers controller, Horse AnimSet |

All logged in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md).

### Claude (Assistant)

Nice, you're fully stocked: about $308 on the Asset Store plus SyntyPass. I've logged everything you own in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md).

Three setup tips before any of it goes into Unity:

1. **Make a separate "asset sandbox" Unity project.** Import packs there to browse and compare styles. The real game project only gets what we actually use. Importing everything into one project bloats it to tens of GBs and slows Unity way down.
2. **Use URP as the render pipeline.** Everything you bought supports it, it's Unity 6's default, and it's what we'll need for the shared shader/palette that makes Synty and polyperfect look like one world. Some packs ship Built-in materials by default, so check for a URP import option or package in each.
3. **SyntyPass details:** the 3-month minimum started today, and you get $10/month store credit. Save that credit in case a non-pass item comes up.

Before the Unity phase, the cheapest high-value test is: Sidekick character creator → build one primitive-looking man and one woman using Goblin/Viking/Villager parts → put them next to a polyperfect deer and an EXPLORER hut. That one screenshot tells us if the style holds together and if the female characters work.

Now for the last planning step, the **first-milestone spec**: the discovery web drawn out, mind model fields, verbs, ecology, and god powers. Since you wanted to draw the web out, I'd make the spec a visual page you can click through rather than a wall of markdown. Want me to start?

### Claude (Assistant)

The spec is up: **[Anthro Milestone One](https://claude.ai/artifact/PDb5MRJGpbbxEvBWuQHcnC)**. The source file is [milestone-1-spec.html](docs/milestone-1-spec.html). Every item is tagged **Decided** (you already agreed), **Proposed** (my suggestion), or **Your call**.

**What's in it:**
- **World:** a ~3 km generated valley with a river, marsh, forest, grassland, and hills, plus deer, wolves, boar, fish, and one megafauna species that can be hunted to extinction.
- **Minds:** the exact fields for each layer, what belief links look like, the choice formula, and the four ways people learn: experience, copying, coincidence (which produces superstition), and culture.
- **Verbs:** about 25 actions in 5 groups, some unlocked by discoveries. *Experiment* is what drives discovery.
- **Discovery web:** 15 tiles, drawn and clickable. Clicking a tile shows its conditions with the OR-routes and highlights its whole chain. The band starts already knowing fire, knapping, spear, and foraging. Pottery is the showcase tile because so much has to line up for it.
- **God:** 6 powers paid for with *favor*, which grows with belief. The **omen** is your main hint tool: you can't tell anyone to try the clay, but you can make the clay bank impossible to ignore.
- **Pass/fail tests for the milestone:** e.g. without your help, pottery shows up in some runs and not others, and by different routes.

**Six questions at the bottom, the main ones being:**
1. Is the starting knowledge right?
2. Do the 15 tiles and their conditions feel true? Add, cut, or rename anything.
3. Does the omen feel like the right way to guide without commanding?
4. **Pace:** in the finished game at normal speed, how long should one generation take to watch?

**Sketches that would help most:** what the **mind view** looks like when you click a person, and what the screen shows when a **life fork** comes up. I designed the systems behind both, but how they *look and feel* is your call. Photos or exports of iPad sketches dropped here work fine.

### Claude (Assistant)

## 1. Hunting: a sequence of steps you can watch

It's not one animation. Every verb is an **activity made of steps**. Each step has its own sim logic, its own chance to go wrong, and its own animation:

| Step | What happens in the sim | What you see |
|---|---|---|
| **Gather** | A hunter recruits others based on friendship, skill, and hunger | People grabbing spears, walking out together |
| **Track** | Find prey using tracking skill + beliefs ("deer at the river in summer") | Walking, crouching to read tracks, pointing |
| **Stalk** | Get close before the animal notices (skill, patience, wind) | Crouch-walking, freezing |
| **Strike** | Throw or thrust (skill, strength, tools) | Throw / thrust animation, the animal reacts |
| **Chase or finish** | Wounded animals run; the bold chase, the cautious hold back | Running, a second throw |
| **Butcher & carry** | Meat, hide, bone, sinew (materials for tiles) | Kneeling work loop, carrying back |
| **Share** | Who gets what feeds status and relationships | Handing out food at camp |

**Animals run their own simple loop** too: graze, watch, flee, fight back. A boar can turn on the hunters, and wolves can show up at the kill. That's where the drama comes from: an injury, a death, a young hunter's first kill becoming a memory and a status boost.

**Group roles:** in a group hunt, people take roles by trait. Bold people strike, patient people drive the prey, kids watch and learn. Watching a hunt is literally how kids learn hunting in the sim.

So **yes, you can watch a whole hunt**, and the Kevin Iglesias pack covers this well (throwing, crouching, carrying, work loops). This is **Proposed**: every verb becomes a sequence of steps, not a single action.

## 2. Is there no more ML at all?

There is ML, it's just not neural networks:
- **The minds learn.** Beliefs update from prediction errors: expected X, got Y, adjust. That's reinforcement learning in its simplest real form.
- **Association learning.** Things that happen together get linked. That's where superstition comes from.
- **Social learning.** People copy successful people, with errors.
- **Evolution.** Inheritance plus selection is a genetic algorithm.

There are **no neural nets, no training phase, no GPU.** Every learner is small, runs live during play, and stays readable for the mind view.

**Two places a heavier ML piece could earn its spot later:**
1. **The prophet translator** (an LLM turns your typed words into commands). Already planned.
2. **Tuning the game.** Use optimization to search thousands of parameter combinations across many simulated runs, to hit the pass/fail tests (e.g. "pottery in 30–60% of runs"). It never ships in the game; it's a development tool that helps a solo dev balance a complex sim.

## 3. The lines: you found real problems

You're right, the diagram was confusing, and some of it was just wrong.

- **Dashed was meant to mean "one of several options," but the other options often aren't tiles.** Bow needs **Cordage AND (Spear-thrower OR long throwing practice)**. "Long throwing practice" is a *skill*, not a tile, so it has no box, and the dashed line looks alone and meaningless.
- **Spear → Dog was a mistake.** Dogs come from meat scraps at camp (a hunting *result*), not from spears. I connected it loosely.

**The fix:** draw **OR-groups explicitly**. A small "any of" junction collects the alternatives, and non-tile options (materials, skills, pressures) show as small chips on it. Every requirement is then visible, and a lone dashed line can't happen. I'll redraw it this way for our tile session.

## 4. Time: three clocks, with a math problem to settle

Your three-clock idea is right, and it's the standard trick:

| Clock | What it governs |
|---|---|
| **Animation clock** | People always move at natural speed, so it never looks fast-forwarded |
| **Day/night clock** | The rhythm you see: sun, sleep, routines |
| **Calendar clock** | Seasons, aging, generations, evolution: what the sim actually runs on |

**The catch:** you said a Planet Zoo-style day/night of ~20 min *and* about 20 min per generation. A generation is ~25 years, so both can't be true if one visual day equals one week:

| If… | then 1 year = | season = | generation = |
|---|---|---|---|
| visual day (20 min) = 1 week | 17 hours | 4 hours | **~18 days of play** |
| generation = 20 min | 48 sec | 12 sec | 20 min |

**My suggestion: decouple day/night from the calendar completely.**
- The sun cycles slowly as ambience, maybe 10–20 min, and people's visible routines follow it (they sleep at visual night).
- Each visual day is a **representative day**. You see a natural-paced slice of village life, while the calendar underneath advances much more.
- **Seasons show gradually:** foliage, snow, and herds moving, not a calendar jump.
- **Generation ≈ 20 min at 1x**, with 1–3x speed like you said.
- When something important happens (a discovery close, a fork, a hunt you're following), the calendar **slows down** so you can watch it play out in full.

**Evolution needs separate handling.** Real visible changes like webbed hands take thousands of years, which at 20 min per generation is hundreds of hours. Two options:
- **Compressed evolution:** selection is exaggerated so traits shift noticeably within ~10–20 generations. Grounded rules, faster outcomes.
- **Deep-time skips:** an optional jump of centuries, with the sim running headless and a summary afterward.

Which feels right? Or both?

## 5. Next: the tile map together

Some framing questions so the session goes well:
1. **Granularity:** should tiles be big ("Pottery") or small steps ("clay noticed → fired figurine → pot → kiln")? Smaller means more discovery moments but a bigger map.
2. **Refinement:** once found, should a tile improve over time (crude pots → good pots), or is it found/not found?
3. **Should materials show on the map** as their own nodes (clay, sinew, reeds), so you can see "clay feeds 4 things"?

After your answers, I'll redraw the web with the new notation, and we'll walk it in batches of ~5 tiles so you can poke at each.

And agreed on sketches: we'll design it together first, and you sketch only if we get stuck.

I've updated the spec at the same link: **[Anthro Milestone One](https://claude.ai/artifact/PDb5MRJGpbbxEvBWuQHcnC)**. Tiles are now big skills, all lines are solid, and clicking a tile shows how it can happen, the **stages inside it**, and what it feeds into. The time section has the three clocks, the calendar pause, and compressed evolution. Everything is logged in [PLANNING.md](Anthro/docs/PLANNING.md).

## Wolves and band strength

Your idea fixes the dog tile and is better anthropology than mine. It matches the leading theory that wolves **domesticated themselves** by hanging around human camps. I've added a **band strength** metric:

- **Inputs:** number of healthy armed adults, spears and later bows, fire, and later fences and palisades.
- **Wolves compare it to their own hunger and pack size.** Strong band and well-fed wolves means they **lurk** and take scraps. Weak band (sickness, men away hunting, a bad winter) or starving wolves means they **raid or attack**.
- **Dogs only come from the lurking zone.** Too weak and wolves are a threat; too aggressive (killing every wolf) and there's nobody left to tame.

So the spear → dog and fire → dog lines now actually mean something: both keep wolves at a respectful distance.

## Evolution: compressed only

Agreed, and I think only compressed. Your feather example is exactly why: a single odd baby with a "feathers, stage 1" flag is a story beat you can follow, while "10,000 years later they have feathers" is just a cutscene. We actually **already have a deep-time skip** for the one place it fits: the **extinction restart**, where how long you wait decides ruins vs. fossils. So no deep-time skips during normal play.

## Tile walkthrough, batch 1 of 4

Open the page, click these, and react to whatever feels off. Here are my specific questions:

**The four starting tiles (Fire, Knapping, Wooden spear, Foraging)**
- These start *known*, but each has **stages inside** that still grow (e.g. Fire → high-heat fires, which pottery needs). Does starting known with room to master feel right?
- Should **language** appear as a fifth starting tile, even though its deep system comes later?

**Cordage**
- It's the root of a lot (baskets, sewing, fishing, bow). The *source* matters: string from **sinew** (hunters) vs **nettle/reeds** (gatherers) leads the culture differently. Do you want that flavor to have real effects later (e.g. sinew cultures get better bows), or just be story text?

**Drying & smoking**
- Usually discovered by **accident**: food left near smoke. Should accidents be a general mechanism? A person's activity creates a chance situation, and a curious person might notice it. That would apply to pottery (clay near the hearth) too.

**Spear-thrower**
- It mainly exists as one route to the bow. Is that enough of a reason for it to be its own tile, or should it fold into the spear's stages?

Answer whichever grab you. Next batch: Grinding, Dog, Burial, Ochre & art, Basketry.
