### Claude (Assistant)

Language is now a starting tile on the [spec page](https://claude.ai/artifact/PDb5MRJGpbbxEvBWuQHcnC). Its stages control how information spreads:
1. **Shared words.** Every discovery adds vocabulary.
2. **Telling, not just showing.** Some skills can then be taught by words alone.
3. **Stories.** Beliefs and lessons outlive the people who lived them.
4. **Ritual speech and song.**

Better language means faster, more faithful teaching. Worse means more copying errors and drift.

## Stages: you're right, they aren't one jump

A tile is really a **family of stages**, and each stage is its own discovery with its own requirements and its own unlocks. Some stages come in **variants** (different routes) that open different doors. Here's Cordage reworked as an example:

| Stage | Requires | Unlocks |
|---|---|---|
| **Sinew thread** (variant) | butchering hunted animals; interested in animals | sewing hides, strong bowstrings, tight lashing on spear points |
| **Plant twine** (variant) | fibrous plants (nettle, reeds); interested in plants | basket lashing, fishing lines |
| **Two-ply string** | *either* variant + some skill | stronger everything: better bows, better hafting |
| **Rope** | **plant twine only**, since sinew is too short | building lashings, rafts, hauling |
| **Knotted nets** | rope + plant twine + water nearby | fishing nets, carrying nets |
| **Sinew-backed bow** | sinew thread + plant twine + a bow (both variants) | stronger bows |

So a **hunter culture with only sinew** sews good clothes and makes great bowstrings, but never gets nets or rafts until someone works with reeds. A **gatherer culture with only twine** fishes and builds rafts but sews poorly. **Both** opens everything plus combo stages. That's the flavor you wanted, and it's real: it's how different peoples ended up with different toolkits.

**The map stays readable:** it still shows big tiles with lines between them. Click a tile and you see its stages, each with its own requirements and unlocks. The lines between tiles are the sum of the links between their stages.

## Accidents: how they differ from normal discovery

| | Normal discovery | Accident |
|---|---|---|
| **Trigger** | someone deliberately experiments or works in that area | an ordinary activity creates a chance situation (clay near the hearth hardens, meat left in smoke dries) |
| **Needs** | all requirements | **only the physical ones:** the materials and the situation. It skips knowledge and pressure |
| **Who notices** | the person doing it | whoever's nearby, if they're curious enough to notice. **Or you, with an omen** |
| **Result** | the stage, learned properly | an **insight**: a belief like "fire hardens clay" plus beginner skill. The deliberate route becomes much more likely |
| **Does it stick?** | yes, if taught | **only if it's used.** Skills fade without practice, and an insight that doesn't fit their way of life fades out |

That last row is the key. An accident can put a band ahead of itself, but it doesn't skip the rest of the chain. A nomadic band can stumble onto fired clay, but they can't keep pots without a base camp, so the knowledge fades. This **really happened**: people at Dolní Věstonice made fired clay figurines around 26,000 years ago, more than 10,000 years before pottery vessels show up anywhere.

**For you as the player:** when an accident happens, you get a notification ("something happened by the hearth") and a short window to omen someone toward it. That's your free jump. Whether it lasts depends on whether the rest of the band's life can support it.

**Some stages are accident-prone:** each stage lists its accident situations, if any. For example, fired clay needs clay worked or left near a hearth, drying needs surplus food near smoke, and dog needs a pup orphaned near camp. Others, like knotted nets, basically can't happen by accident.

## Next step

If this stage model and the accident rules sound right, I'll **rewrite all 20 tiles** this way (stage-level requirements, variants, unlocks, and accident situations), update the page to show them, and then we walk them in batches. That rewrite *is* the real design of the discovery system, so it's the most important thing we've done so far. Does the Cordage example look like what you meant?

### Claude (Assistant)

The full discovery web is rewritten and live at the same link: **[Anthro Milestone One](https://claude.ai/artifact/PDb5MRJGpbbxEvBWuQHcnC)**, section 5.

**What changed**
- **20 skills, 75 stages.** Each stage has its own needs, what it does, and what it opens. 15 stages can happen by accident; they have an ochre dot.
- **The map is now skill lanes.** Each row is a skill, and stages sit left to right by how many steps they are from the start. Skills had to stop being single columns, because Base camp starts at step 2 and ends at step 5. The deepest stage, keeping the best seed, is 7 steps out.
- Click a stage to light up its whole chain in both directions. The panel lets you jump between linked stages, or click a skill name to see all its stages.
- **The data lives in its own file,** [discovery-web.json](Anthro/docs/discovery-web.json), and that same file will feed the sim. The page rebuilds from it with `python build-spec.py`. I checked it: every reference points at a real stage, and there are no circular requirements.

**Design calls I made that you should check**
- **Cordage routes:** sinew gives sewing and bowstrings, plant twine gives baskets and fishing lines, and rope and nets need twine. The sinew-backed bow needs both routes.
- **Fired clay is reachable at step 2, through an accident by the hearth.** Pinch pots need long stays at a base camp. So a nomadic band can find fired clay early and make figurines, but it fades unless they settle. That's the Dolní Věstonice story.
- **Dogs:** "wolves lurk" is a *situation*, not a discovery. It needs band strength, so your deterrence idea is built in. The main way to a pup in camp is an accident: an orphaned pup.
- **Language:** stories make lore outlive people (plant lore needs them), ritual song needs ancestor rites, and tally marks come from tracking stores.
- **Burning the land** has an accident tied to your lightning, so an omen can teach fire management.

**Walkthrough, batch 1**

Let's start with the five starting skills and Cordage, since everything else hangs off them. Click through Fire, Knapping, Spear, Foraging, Language, and Cordage, and tell me:
1. Does anything feel **out of order**, like something that should come earlier or later?
2. Is anything **missing** that a Stone Age band would obviously figure out?
3. Do the **accidents** feel natural, or are any of them a stretch?

The web is updated at the same link, **[Anthro Milestone One](https://claude.ai/artifact/PDb5MRJGpbbxEvBWuQHcnC)**, and it's now **24 skills and 96 stages**. The build checks passed: every reference resolves and there are no circular requirements.

**You were right about the bow.** Snares didn't exist, so the accident had no source. Now there's a **Trapping** skill: game trails → snares → deadfalls → **spring-pole snares**. The bow's accident is "a spring-pole snare snaps back and flings its trigger stick across the trail." Spring snares also *help* the bow normally, since they're the idea of stored spring energy.

**New skills**
- **Bone & antler:** bone awls → antler spear points (useful where flint is scarce) → **eyed needles** → fish hooks → barbed harpoons. Fitted clothing now needs bone needles, awl stitching needs bone awls, and hook-and-line fishing needs bone hooks.
- **Woodworking:** shaping wood (known) → hafted stone axes → ground and polished axes → **dugout canoes**. Since this is an island game, the canoe is the first step toward reaching other islands. It's deep: polished axes plus high-heat fires to burn and scrape the log.
- **Shelter:** windbreaks (known) → **cave living** (you have to drive out bears or wolves, so it needs band strength) → lean-tos → pole-and-thatch huts → pit houses. A year-round base camp now accepts hide tents, huts, *or* pit houses.

**Additions to existing skills**
- **Footwear** in Hide: fewer injuries and frostbite, faster travel.
- **Torches** and **stone fat lamps** in Fire. **Cave paintings now need light** to work deep inside a cave, which is authentic and ties three skills together. Handprints now need cave living.

**One thing to confirm:** the new skills come with two more **known-at-start** stages, *shaping wood* (spears already imply it) and *windbreaks*. That slightly extends the starting knowledge you approved. OK?

**Next batch:** the middle of the web, meaning Drying, Grinding, Dog, Burial, Ochre & art, Basketry, and Storage. The same three questions apply: anything out of order, anything missing, any accident that feels like a stretch?

### Claude (Assistant)

Here are both in detail. They're connected: one trait I'm proposing (credulity) is what makes the favor economy work.

# Part 1: Favor, the god's currency

## Where favor comes from

Favor comes from **acts of belief**, not just from belief existing:

| Source | How much | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Prayer** | small, each time someone prays | The steady income. Stronger belief means more favor per prayer |
| **Rituals** | a burst, scaled by how many take part | Group rites, burials with offerings, festivals |
| **Offerings** | a medium burst | Food, tools, figures left for you. Costs the band something real |
| **Sacrifice** | a large burst | Animal sacrifice is a bit "cruel." Human sacrifice is a big shift toward **cruel**, and innocent deaths cost believers, as you decided earlier |
| **Baseline trickle** | tiny, always | So you can never get permanently stuck at zero |

## The core loop: prayers are requests

When someone prays, they're usually **asking for something**: "Ren prays for the rain to come," "Asha prays her fever breaks." You see these as notifications.

- **Answer it** (spend favor on rain) and their belief grows, and so does the belief of everyone who hears about it. You spend favor to earn more favor.
- **Ignore it** and their faith weakens a little. Unanswered prayers are how **doubt** is born.
- **Coincidences count.** If it rains on its own after a prayer, you get the credit for free. If a disaster follows a ritual, you get the blame. That's the misinterpretation mechanic paying off in your economy.

## Limits

- **Favor cap:** your pool can't exceed a limit that grows with the number of believers, so you can't hoard for 200 years and then rain miracles.
- **Faith decay:** belief fades slowly without signs, answered prayers, or rituals. You have to stay present.
- **Doubt spreads:** skeptics (stubborn, low credulity, or burned by unanswered prayers) pass doubt along just like other beliefs. That's your atheism.

## Two meters, not one

1. **Favor** is what you spend.
2. **Reputation**, from *kind* to *cruel*, is never spent. It's the band's shared belief about what you're like, built from what follows your signs. A storm right after an omen says "angry god"; a herd arriving after a prayer says "generous god."
   - A **kind** reputation makes blessings cheaper (blessed births, good weather, sending herds).
   - A **cruel** reputation makes wrath cheaper (storms, plagues, cold) and makes people offer more out of fear, but more fear means more sacrifice.
   - This is where malevolent vs. benevolent gods get different powers.

## Starting costs (placeholder numbers, to be tuned)

| Power | Cost | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Omen | 5 | Cheap on purpose: it's your main hint tool |
| Nudge a fork | 10 | Only when offered |
| Shape land (small: a clay bank, an ochre seam) | 20 | |
| Shape land (large: a grove, a reed bed) | 40 | |
| Send animals | 30 | Bigger herds cost more |
| Weather, one season | 60 | Your biggest regular power |
| Blessed birth | 40 | Plus a limit per generation |

**Start:** a pool of about 50, since nobody knows you exist yet. Your first job is to get noticed with omens.

**Questions:**
1. Should **losing all believers** be a loss, or just a hard state you climb out of with the baseline trickle?
2. Is the **reputation** meter (kind ↔ cruel, shifting what's cheap) the right way to make malevolent and benevolent play differently?

# Part 2: Traits and interests

## Traits

I'd revise the earlier list: **caution and boldness were the same axis twice**, so I merged them, and I added three that the game's systems specifically need.

**Mind traits (inherited, fixed for life, each 0–1)**

| Trait | Low ↔ High | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Curiosity** | sticks to the known ↔ tries new things | Chooses *experiment* and *explore*; notices accidents. The main discovery trait |
| **Boldness** | cautious ↔ reckless | Risky hunts, chasing wounded prey, fighting. Cautious people weigh bad outcomes double, plan ahead, and store food |
| **Sociability** | loner ↔ social | Socializing, joining group hunts; copies others more. Social people spread knowledge |
| **Patience** | restless ↔ patient | Long tasks: weaving, sewing, trapping, grinding. Gets better results from slow work |
| **Openness** | stubborn ↔ open | How fast beliefs change. Stubborn people keep traditions (and superstitions); open people adapt |
| **Temper** *(new)* | gentle ↔ aggressive | Conflict, dominance, attitude toward wolves. Matters for dogs now and war later |
| **Warmth** *(new)* | cold ↔ caring | Sharing food, caring for kids and the sick, teaching willingly. Shapes how a band treats its weakest |
| **Credulity** *(new)* | skeptic ↔ believer | How easily coincidence becomes belief. **The trait your religion runs on**: credulous people see your hand everywhere, skeptics spread doubt |

**Body traits (inherited, each 0–1)**

| Trait | What it does |
|---|---|
| **Strength** | Hunting, building, carrying, fighting |
| **Dexterity** | Knapping, sewing, cordage, fine crafts |
| **Endurance** | Long hunts, travel, surviving illness and hunger |
| **Cold tolerance** | Surviving winter. The first trait that can **evolve** under pressure |

Body traits are where evolution happens. Later pathways (fur, webbing, breath-holding) get added here as more body traits.

**How they're inherited:** each child gets the average of their parents plus random drift, and sometimes a bigger jump. A blessed birth lets you set them directly.

## Interests

Nine domains, mapped to skills so every skill has interests that pull people toward it:

| Interest | Pulls toward |
|---|---|
| **Stone & earth** | knapping, grinding, clay and pottery, ochre |
| **Wood** *(new)* | woodworking, shelter, bows, spear-throwers |
| **Plants** | foraging, twine, baskets, wild grain, plant lore |
| **Animals** | hunting, trapping, hide, bone, dogs |
| **Water** | fishing, harpoons, canoes |
| **Fire** | fire mastery, drying, firing clay, torches |
| **People** | socializing, teaching, stories, leading |
| **Spirits** | burial, ritual, prayer, ochre art |
| **Sky** | weather, seasons, tally marks, reading omens |

I added **Wood** because a whole cluster of skills (shelter, woodworking, bows) had no interest pulling people toward them.

**How interests grow:**
- **Childhood matters most.** Kids absorb the interests of whoever raises and teaches them, and early experiences hit hardest. A kid who sees a mammoth hunt at age 6 may become a hunter for life.
- **Enjoyment:** doing something and succeeding raises interest in it.
- **Events:** seeing an omen raises Spirits and Sky interest.
- Interests **drift slowly**, so adults rarely change completely.

**Questions:**
3. Do the three new traits (**temper, warmth, credulity**) earn their place? Credulity is the one I'd fight for.
4. Should there be a **learning speed** trait (some people pick things up faster)? I left it out because curiosity and patience already cover much of it, and it gets close to "smart vs. dumb" people. But it would make specialists and prodigies more distinct.
5. Are the **body traits** right? Anything else you'd want to be able to evolve early?

### Claude (Assistant)

Logged and updating the spec. Here's where each one landed.

## Faith collapse: last chances instead of a trickle

Agreed, a trickle at zero believers doesn't make sense. Here's a version built on your idea:

- **The spiral is natural:** fewer believers → less favor → fewer answered prayers → more doubt → fewer believers. You'll feel it slipping before it hits bottom.
- **Desperate prayers are your last chances.** When believers get very low, a crisis (famine, sickness, an attack) can make someone pray who hasn't in years, usually a credulous person. Each comes with a **last ember**: just enough favor to answer that one prayer. A miracle in a desperate moment gives a **much bigger** belief boost than normal, so one well-timed answer can bring back a few believers.
- **You get about three of these.** If you miss or waste them and belief hits zero, **you fade**. The game is lost: humanity carries on without you, and you're forgotten. Extinction is the other loss.
- **Effect on society:** shared rituals hold a band together, so losing them makes the band more fragile. But that bonus comes from *any* shared rites (ancestor rites count), not specifically from worshipping you, so the game doesn't preach that non-believers fall apart.

Reputation as a Red Dead–style honor meter: logged as decided.

## Traits: final list

| | Trait | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Mind** | Curiosity | experimenting, noticing accidents |
| | Boldness | risk vs. caution |
| | Sociability | joins in, copies others |
| | Patience | slow, careful work |
| | Openness | how fast beliefs change |
| | Temper | aggression, dominance |
| | Warmth | caring, sharing, teaching willingly |
| | Credulity | how easily coincidence becomes belief |
| | **Charisma** *(new)* | how much others copy, follow, and believe them. Makes leaders, storytellers, and prophets. Ideas from charismatic people spread faster, true or not |
| | **Aptitude** *(new)* | how fast skills improve, from practice and from being taught. This is your "intelligence," framed as how quickly someone learns, so a patient, low-aptitude person can still become a master |
| | **Resilience** *(new)* | how well they take grief, fear, and hardship. Low resilience means panic in danger and despair after loss; high means they keep working through a famine |
| **Body** | Strength, Dexterity, Endurance, Cold tolerance | as before |
| | **Height** *(new)* | reach, speed, food needs |
| | **Build** *(new)* | stocky ↔ slender. Stocky bodies hold heat (cold tolerance) but eat more. This is a real biological rule, and it means climate will visibly shape body types over generations |

Height and build show up directly on the Sidekick characters through body blend shapes.

## Mate selection: include it, in a simple form

I'd include it now, because **evolution depends on who has children**:
- Pairs form from **relationship + attraction + availability**. Close kin are excluded, and customs (monogamy, etc.) shape it later.
- **Attraction is partly cultural.** Each band develops shared preferences, like "good hunters," "strong," or "skilled with their hands." This is **sexual selection**, and it can drive evolution: a culture that prizes some odd trait will spread it faster. That's one way your feather people could happen.
- Kept tasteful: pair bonds and children, nothing explicit.

## Interests

**Add Art now:** body paint, figures, cave paintings, songs, and decoration all exist in this milestone, and right now they're squeezed into Spirits. That makes 10 interests.

**Science and philosophy wait** for later societies with specialists and writing. Sky (watching seasons and stars, tallying moons) is the early version of science.

### Claude (Assistant)

Good call to stop. I'll answer your two questions, then give the full picture. One background test run is still going; nothing depends on it.

## Your two questions

### Is mammoth avoidance a naturally learned belief?

Yes. Every person carries a belief, "how dangerous are mammoths," and it changes in three ways:
- **Instinct at birth:** big, aggressive animals look dangerous before any experience.
- **Their own hunts:** getting hurt, or a near miss, raises it fast. A calm hunt lowers it only slowly. Cautious people raise it more.
- **Other people:** everyone hears how someone died, and it hits hardest for those who loved them. Beliefs also spread by talk, weighted toward charismatic and respected people. So a band that's lost hunters to mammoths ends up with a shared fear, and their children inherit it through stories.

When a party picks a target, each person weighs, for themselves:

> meat (more when the band is hungry, less if it would rot) + prestige (pulls the bold) − fear × (how bad a wound would be + chance it kills) − walking distance on tired legs

against just going home. The group follows influence-weighted opinion, and people who disagree and don't defer to the leader walk off.

So "avoid mammoths unless desperate" emerges from hunger raising the meat side. **"Unless in a big group" only half works**: party size makes a kill more feasible, but doesn't yet lower each person's own risk. That's a real gap to fix.

### What exactly changed in moving camp?

It went through four versions, and you should see the path, because I was iterating without checking in:

| Version | How it worked | Problem |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Every 7 days, compare random sites, move if 1.8× better | Hard-coded; nobody decided anything |
| 2 | At the fire, someone who remembers a better place proposes it; adults weigh it by their own memory or trust in the proposer; influence decides | Ping-ponged constantly: people judged their current camp by stale general experience, and moving cost nothing |
| 3 | Memories are per place **and season**; current camp judged by remembered nearby places; gain must exceed the effort of moving (attachment, weariness from the last move, carrying kids and elders) | Zero moves: nobody ever saw anywhere else |
| 4 (running now) | Curious people sometimes forage farther afield, so the band learns its land | Results pending |

## Where we are against the milestone plan

| Planned for milestone 1 | Status |
|---|---|
| Generated valley, seasons, plants, climate | ✅ Done |
| Animals and ecology | ✅ Done, and expanded (see below) |
| Minds: traits, interests, skills | ✅ All 11 mind traits, 7 body traits (Immunity added), 10 interests |
| Minds: beliefs that learn from experience, coincidence, and others | 🟡 **Partial.** There's learning from experience and others for activities, danger, and places. The belief **graph** with coincidence-based superstition, customs, and taboos isn't built yet |
| Verbs as watchable step sequences | 🟡 **Partial.** Forage, hunt (full sequence), knap, flint trips, care, teach-by-watching, socialize, rest, sleep, play. Missing: **experiment**, ritual, tend fire, build shelter |
| Discovery web | 🔴 **Loaded and validated only.** Nothing can be discovered yet, there are no accidents, and no knowledge passes between people. This is the heart of the milestone, and it isn't started |
| God powers, favor, belief in you | 🔴 Not started |
| Births, pairing, inheritance, aging, death | ✅ Done, all causal |
| Chronicle + yearly summary | ✅ Done |
| Tests | ✅ 9 passing |
| 100 years in under 10 seconds | 🔴 Currently ~25 s, and probably worse after the latest change. Needs optimization |

## Things I added that weren't in the plan

These came out of your "structural, not hard-coded" push, but they're real design additions, and **you haven't approved them**:

1. **Regional populations and migration.** The valley is one corner of a wider region. Herds breed region-wide, come in each spring at a random natural rate, and migratory ones leave in autumn. A dangerous valley gets avoided for a few years. Killing animals here drains the region.
2. **Animal fear.** Hunted herds get warier and keep away from camp.
3. **Physical hunting.** Weapon range vs. how early animals bolt, hits vs. size, wounded animals charging. This makes the spear-thrower and bow matter mechanically.
4. **A body and disease model.** No random death rolls. Every death traces to hunger, cold, wounds, sickness (spread by contact, crowding, rotten meat, infected wounds), childbirth, or an aging body losing capacity. Infants live on their mother's milk.
5. **Neighboring bands** wander the wider region, and their people visit when nearby. That's where partners from outside come from. Their own lives aren't simulated yet.
6. **Courtship** through socializing, with attraction shaped by band culture.
7. **Prestige** as a motive pulling bold hunters toward big game.

## Results right now

- Unguided bands generally **survive 150 years** at 25–70 people, a realistic forager pattern.
- **Outcomes differ:** in some runs mammoths are hunted to regional extinction, in others they persist.
- **Problems:**
  - Mammoth injuries are still too common.
  - **Food is almost always plentiful.** That's a problem for discovery: drying, storage, and the rest are driven by pressure, and there's hardly any.

## Still hard-coded or hand-set (honest audit)

- **Fixed schedules:** meals at 7am and 7pm. A 2-day rest between hunts (should come from fatigue and recovery). Neighbors "renewing" their people each year.
- **Hand-picked numbers everywhere:** how much people fear injury vs. value meat, learning rates, disease rates. The *structure* is causal, but the numbers are my guesses. The planned optimization tool for tuning is how these get set properly.
- **Personal values could be traits:** how much someone values their own safety vs. glory is currently the same for everyone.

## What I'd suggest next

1. **You approve or adjust the 7 additions** above.
2. I fix performance, then stop tuning ecology.
3. **Build the discovery system**: experiment, accidents, teaching and losing knowledge. That's what the milestone is actually about, and right now I'm polishing the stage it plays on.
4. Then god powers, favor, and belief formation.

I can also write all of this into PLANNING.md as a "how the sim works" section so it's on record. Where do you want to go from here?

I've logged all 7 additions as approved in [PLANNING.md](docs/PLANNING.md), along with a principle we've been circling: **nothing scheduled, nothing global.**

## Hand-picked numbers: you're right that it partly exists already

Some of what you describe is already in:
- **Boldness** lowers fear and raises the pull of glory.
- **Resilience** dulls fear.
- **Aptitude and openness** set how fast someone learns.
- **Immunity, age, and hunger** set how well someone fights off sickness.

But plenty of numbers are still one value for everyone. The key is separating two kinds of number:

**1. World facts:** how fast meat rots, how big a mammoth is, how contagious a fever is. These aren't anyone's choice. They should be grounded in real data where it exists, and the tuning tool should check the sim against real-world patterns, like forager death rates by age or how often real hunts succeed.

**2. Valuations:** how much someone fears injury vs. wants meat, how much they value glory, how they weigh their own life. These should **never be global**. Right now "an injury is worth 60 rations, death is worth 400" is the same for everyone. Instead, each person's weights would come from three things:

| Source | Example |
|---|---|
| **Traits** | Bold people fear less; warm people weigh harm to others more |
| **Situation** | A parent of small children values their own life far more; a starving person accepts more risk; an old hunter has less to lose |
| **Culture** | Band-wide norms like *valor*, *caution*, or *sharing*, learned and spread like any belief. A band that lost three hunters to mammoths drifts cautious; one that celebrates big kills drifts toward valor |

Meal times and hunting cadence work the same way: people eat when hungry, and hunt again when they've recovered and the band needs meat. Over time a band can develop a **shared rhythm as a custom**, because people copy what others do.

## Disease: half there

What exists: spread from how many are sick and how crowded the camp is, rotten meat, infected wounds. Who gets sick depends on immunity, age, hunger, and past illness, and recovery depends on care and rest.

What it should become:
- **Diseases as actual things,** each with its own contagiousness, severity, and duration. A cold, a deadly fever, a gut sickness.
- **Spread through real contact:** whoever you talked to, slept beside, or cared for. A healer caring for the sick gets exposed. Visitors from neighboring bands can bring something new that your band has no resistance to.
- Later, when people settle and crowd together (base camp), disease naturally gets worse, which is real anthropology.

## Medicine: yes, it belongs in the web

It fits naturally, since care and plant lore already exist. Here's a draft **Healing** skill:

| Stage | Needs | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Wound care** | caring skill, moss or hides | Cleaning and binding wounds; fewer infections |
| **Splinting** | wound care, cordage | Broken bones heal instead of crippling |
| **Herbal remedies** | plant lore | Fevers and gut sickness hurt less |
| **Antiseptics** | herbal remedies, resin or honey | Infections become rare |
| **Midwifery** | caring skill, experience at births | Childbirth deaths drop sharply |
| **Bone setting & surgery** | splinting, fine blades | Survive wounds that would kill; trepanation really existed in the Stone Age |

Each one removes a **specific cause of death the sim already tracks**, so the band would *feel* the difference. A **healer** role would emerge naturally, often tied to the ritual specialist.

## Questions before I build

1. **Valuations:** does traits + situation + culture sound right? And should culture norms be a fixed set (valor, caution, sharing, …), or fully open-ended beliefs?
2. **Disease:** are named, distinct diseases spreading through actual contact the level you want?
3. **Healing:** add it to the web as drafted, or change it?

**Proposed order:**
1. Fix performance and the two bugs (one-way partner moves, party size not sharing risk).
2. Valuations + culture norms + natural meal and hunting rhythms.
3. Disease rework.
4. Then the discovery system itself, including Healing.

### Claude (Assistant)

Added **Healing** as an 11th interest. It'll pull people toward caring for the sick, plant lore, and the Healing skill, and it's how healers emerge. Here's how I plan to do the two fixes.

## One-way partner moves

**Why it happens:** to decide who moves, I compare each person's ties to their own people. Neighboring bands aren't simulated day to day, so their people never built friendships. A visitor's ties come out as zero, and they always move.

**The fix:**
- **Neighbors get real relationships when they're created:** parents, siblings, children, partners, and baseline fondness for people they grew up with. Their ties become as real as ours.
- **Ties are weighted by what they are:** your own children count most (nobody leaves their kids), then parents and siblings, then friends.
- **The couple decides together, like any pair would:** each weighs what they'd lose by leaving (their ties) against what they'd gain (their partner's people, and how well-off each band seems). Whoever has less to lose moves.
- **A residence custom can form from this.** If, by chance, women usually move to their husbands' band, that pattern spreads as a shared expectation, the same way any belief spreads. It's a culture-level norm, as we just agreed, not something I set.

## Party size and risk

**Why it fails:** when a wounded animal charges, every hunter currently rolls to get hurt, so a party of 6 is no safer per person than a party of 2. And people don't factor party size into their fear.

**The fix, following the physical reality:**
- **A charging animal goes for one person, maybe two for a mammoth,** usually the nearest or boldest. It doesn't hit everyone.
- **Everyone else gets a strike in during the charge.** More hunters means more spears in the animal, so a bigger party is more likely to drop it or turn it before it does real harm, and can drag the injured person clear.
- **People reason about this:** what they learn is "how often a charge from this animal hurts someone," and their *personal* risk is that divided by how many others are there to share it. So "only hunt mammoths in a big group" becomes something people **learn and act on**, and bands that try it with 2 people get punished for it.

Building these now, along with the performance fix and the Healing interest.

I hit the usage limit, so I stopped partway through step 3. Everything is logged in `docs/PROGRESS.md`, and nothing is committed to git.

**Step 1: performance and bug fixes (done).**
- A 100-year run now takes about 6–15 seconds instead of 130–450. That's still above the 10-second target for bigger bands.
- Partners now move between bands in both directions.
- In a hunt, the charging animal goes for one or two people instead of everyone, so bigger parties really are safer per person, and people take that into account.
- New: when times are hard for a long stretch or the camp gets too big, a band can split. The group that leaves becomes a neighboring band.
- Added Healing as an interest. Tests passed (9 of 9).

**Step 2: personal values, culture, natural rhythms (done).**
- People eat when they're hungry instead of at set mealtimes. Foragers snack in the field and hunters eat at the kill. The fixed gap between hunts is gone.
- Two culture values, valor and sharing, that people learn from what happens to them and pass on in conversation.
- How much someone fears injury or values their own life now depends on their traits, their situation (parents of small children are more careful), and their band's culture.
- Results: mammoth deaths fell from about 40 to about 10 per century. Winter hunger is now the main pressure, which is what drying and storage discoveries are meant to solve. Tests passed (9 of 9).

**Step 3: disease (built, not finished).** Six distinct diseases now spread only through real contact. Colds and fevers arrive from neighboring bands, and the gut sickness comes from a fouled camp, which pushes bands to move.
- **Open problem:** deaths from starvation and cold roughly tripled after this change. My guess is that sick people stop foraging and illness spreads through sleeping families, so the band runs short of food. I haven't confirmed that yet.
- **I haven't re-run the tests since step 3.**

**Not started:** step 4, the discovery system (experimenting, accidents, teaching and losing knowledge, and the Healing skill).

**Decisions I made that you should check** (each marked [call] in the log):
- Band splitting, and the minimum of 12 people who must leave or stay.
- Couples develop a band custom about who moves to whose band, starting with no preference.
- Starting with two culture values (valor and sharing), with caution folded into valor as one daring-to-cautious scale.
- Leaving out a "people eat together at the fire" custom for now.

When you're back, I'd start by running the tests, then dig into the starvation spike, then move to the discovery system.

### Claude (Assistant)

### Claude (Assistant)

## Why can't bands swell to 165?

**They can.** There's no cap, and I didn't add any decay to ties. Two separate things happened:

1. **The swelling came from a bug plus a missing mechanism.** People could marry *into* the band but never *out* of it, and bands had no way to split. So bands only grew until famine knocked them down.
2. **The "ties" change wasn't decay.** When a couple from two bands decides who moves, each person weighs what they'd leave behind. I was summing affinity to *everyone* in the band, so someone in a 165-person band always looked more tied than someone in a 25-person one, even with a few close friends. Now it counts each person's ~8 closest bonds (children weighted most). Nothing decays; it just measures attachment the way people experience it.

What now limits band size is people's own choices. During long hunger, or when most of the camp are near-strangers, someone may propose leaving and others decide whether to follow. That near-stranger effect is what anthropologists call *scalar stress*, and it's a real reason mobile foragers mostly lived in bands of about 25–50.

**Two caveats:**
- How strongly crowding is felt is a number I picked, so it's hand-set like other things we've talked about.
- More importantly, **this must not stop villages from forming later.** Real societies grew past band size by inventing ways to manage scalar stress: leaders, clans, rituals, shared identity. So crowding should be reducible by culture and institutions. That's a great hook: hierarchy and clans can emerge as a *solution* to a pressure the sim already has.

## How culture and norms work right now

**What a norm is:** each person holds a 0–1 belief for each norm. There are only two right now:

| Norm | Low ↔ High |
|---|---|
| **valor** | cautious ↔ daring |
| **sharing** | hold on to your own ↔ share with everyone |

**Where people's values come from:**
- **Founding:** each band starts with its own shade of these values, with personal variation.
- **Experience:**
  - Valor: a clean kill of something dangerous says daring pays, someone hurt says it doesn't, and a death to an animal pushes hard toward caution (most for those who loved the dead).
  - Sharing: it rises in famines and after starvation deaths, and loosens in easy times.
- **Conversation:** people copy each other, weighted toward charismatic and respected speakers.
- **Childhood:** children absorb the values of the adults they watch.
- **Newcomers:** partners from other bands bring their band's values.

**What norms actually change:**
- Valor: how much people fear injury, how much they value their own life, and how much they chase glory in hunts.
- Sharing: how much people hold back when eating from shared food in hard times.

**You're right that it barely matters.** That's the whole effect. Valor only varied between about 0.36 and 0.49 across bands, and sharing has one narrow use. Culture right now is a couple of dials on two decisions, not something that makes bands feel different.

### What I think culture should be

**1. More dimensions that touch real decisions:**

| Norm | What it would shape |
|---|---|
| Valor | hunting risk (exists) |
| Sharing | who gets food, hoarding, stores (exists, but needs more uses) |
| **Openness to the new vs. tradition** | whether people experiment and adopt discoveries. *Central for the discovery system.* |
| **Hospitality vs. wariness of outsiders** | visits, marriages across bands, later conflict |
| **Mobility vs. attachment to place** | how often they move; the road to base camps and villages |
| **Equality vs. deference to status** | whose voice counts in decisions. Today influence weights are fixed |
| **Kin-first vs. band-wide obligation** | who you share with and help |
| Spirituality | how readily the band forms beliefs about you, the god |

**2. Open-ended customs, not only dials.** Real culture is mostly *specific practices*: "we eat together at dusk," "we don't hunt mammoths," "we bury our dead by the river," "women don't knap." The mechanism:
- When many people do the same thing in the same situation, others copy it.
- Once it's common, people who break it lose a little standing (affinity and respect).
- It becomes self-reinforcing, and it can be passed on long after the original reason is gone.

That's also exactly how superstitions about you would form: someone prays, it rains, others copy, and it becomes custom. It unifies norms, customs, taboos, and religion into one system.

**3. Real weight in decisions.** The planned "custom / taboo" term in the activity scoring would come from these, so culture would shape what people actually *do* every day, not just two risk calculations.

**4. Sources of variation:** different founding shades; drift from imperfect copying (each generation re-learns slightly differently); divergent histories; charismatic individuals swinging the band; outsiders; and later, your omens and prophets.

## What the "flux rates" are

The flux is dysentery-type gut illness, historically a major killer of young children.

- **Filth** builds around camp: +0.002 per person per day, decaying 2% a day. A band of 30 that never moves settles at about 3.
- **Daily chance of catching it** = filth × 0.0004 (doubled for children under 5), reduced by immunity and by recent flux immunity. For that band of 30, that's about 0.4 cases a year per adult and ~0.9 per small child, *if they never move*. Moving camp resets filth to zero.
- **Once sick:** severity grows 5% a day (×1.5 for small children) while the immune response builds, and does damage in proportion to severity. Recovery leaves weak, short-lived protection.

**Honest caveat:** these are calibrated guesses, not data. My first numbers were about 10× too high, and the flux killed 604 people across 6 runs. They should be checked against real data, like child mortality from gut illness in mobile vs. settled foragers. That's the "world facts grounded in real data" job we agreed on.

## Couples splitting up

**Right now there's none.** Couples only end when someone dies.

**I think there should be.** Separation was common among real foragers: the Hadza and !Kung have high divorce rates, especially early in a marriage, and serial monogamy is typical. It matters for the sim because it affects:
- who raises children (step-parents, mothers with kids moving between bands)
- fertility and who has children with whom (which feeds evolution)
- who goes when a band splits
- drama: the kind of personal story Fallout Shelter-level characters need

**How it could work naturally:** the bond between partners keeps changing with time together, care, conflict (temper), neglect, failing to provide, childlessness, and attraction to someone else. When it erodes enough and a better option exists, they separate. How easily that happens is **cultural**: a band can develop norms that make separation easy, frowned on, or rare. Who keeps the children could also be a custom that forms from practice, like the residence custom already does.

## Questions for you

1. Does **culture as open-ended customs plus a set of core value dimensions** sound right? It's a bigger change, but it's what gives culture real weight and variety.
2. Should I add **couple separation** as described?
3. Should **crowding be reducible by culture and institutions** (leaders, clans, shared rituals), so villages can form later?

Pending from before: the starvation spike from step 3 still needs investigating, and the tests need re-running.

### Claude (Assistant)

Good question, and checking it properly changed my answer.

**Is "tripled" even real?** Probably not as I stated it. My step-2 count left out deaths of children under 5, because those are logged as minor events and I didn't include them. The step-3 tally *did* include them. So I compared two different things. I'll recount fairly before calling it a problem.

**Is lots of death a problem in itself?** No. Early humans died a lot: roughly 40–60% of foragers died before 15, mostly from illness. What matters is whether the **mix of causes** and the **overall population trend** look like real forager life. From studies of modern foragers (Gurven & Kaplan 2007), roughly:

| Cause | Share of deaths |
|---|---|
| Illness (infections, gut, respiratory) | ~70% |
| Violence | ~10–20% (varies a lot by group) |
| Accidents: hunting, falls, drowning, animals | ~5–10% |
| Old age / degenerative | ~10%, mostly elders |
| Outright starvation | rare as a direct cause; hunger mostly kills by weakening people against illness |

If our sim shows starvation and cold far outweighing illness, it has the mix **inverted**, and that's the real problem worth fixing, not the raw count.

**Hunts and conflict:**
- **Hunting deaths exist:** trampled by mammoths, gored by boar, and wolf attacks on people.
- **Conflict between people doesn't exist yet.** No fights, feuds, raids, or murder. It's in scope (we agreed wars and violence can happen but are avoidable), but not built. So the sim is currently missing a 10–20% slice of real deaths entirely. It belongs with the culture work, since temper, norms about outsiders, and disputes all feed into it.

Recounting fairly now, by cause and age.

### Claude (Assistant)

Two things: an answer to your immunity question, then a deeper problem the new numbers exposed.

## Your immunity question: mostly yes, and part of it exists already

- **Base robustness** is the inherited Immunity trait. It stays.
- **Per-disease immunity after recovery already exists** (antibodies, essentially). Each disease leaves its own protection, fading at its own rate: strong and long for fever, weak and short for colds.
- **What to add:**
  1. **Childhood exposure and maternal antibodies.** Infants get some protection through breast milk, and kids who survive a disease are protected long-term. That's why "people are immune to their own diseases."
  2. **Inherited resistance to specific diseases.** Over generations of exposure, populations evolve resistance to their *local* diseases. Separately from base robustness, this is real biology (sickle-cell vs. malaria, for example).
  3. **New diseases.** Occasionally a disease emerges from animals or arrives from far-off contact, and **nobody** has resistance. This is the smallpox scenario. It ties directly into your founding question about colonization: when two long-separated populations meet, the one without the other's diseases gets devastated. It would fall out of the system naturally.
- **Medicine and witch doctors fit perfectly, through the same mechanism as religion.** People try remedies (a plant, a chant, a charm) and learn from what happens after. Since most illnesses pass on their own, **useless rituals get credited** for recoveries, spread as beliefs, and earn the healer prestige. Remedies that actually work, like real plants from the Healing skill, help for real. So you'd get a mix of genuine medicine and superstition, and the player can't easily tell which is which, just like the people. Healers emerge from the Healing interest and the prestige of "cures."

I'll add all of this to the plan.

## The deeper problem the numbers just showed

**Starvation is now 61% of deaths, mostly among adults**, and child deaths dropped to 17%, which is too low. The cause is structural: **bodies had no fat reserves.** "Nourishment" measured how often someone's stomach was empty, so a hunter out for 10 hours counted as starving even though real people go days without food. Fixing it properly:
- **Body reserves:** the stomach burns first, then fat. Eating well when food is plentiful builds reserves, including an autumn fattening, which is real.
- **Nourishment reflects reserves:** about 3+ days' worth means well-nourished. Starvation only happens when reserves run out after a long shortage.

That should make hunger a slow, visible pressure that mostly kills *through* illness, which is what you described.

### Claude (Assistant)

Foraging is now density-dependent and calorie-weighted. Tests pass (9/9), and the log is updated. I'm going to stop tuning here, because what's left is a design decision for you, not a bug.

## Cooking: yes, it's related, and it helps

People didn't survive fine before cooking in the sense you might think. Cooking is roughly **a million years older than our species**, and some researchers argue it shaped human evolution (smaller guts, bigger brains). Our band knows fire from day one, so they'd absolutely cook. The sim just doesn't model it yet. It would matter in three concrete ways:
- **More food from the same harvest.** Cooking roots, grain, and meat unlocks a lot more usable energy, roughly 30% or more for starchy foods. That directly eases the winter squeeze.
- **Safer food.** Cooked meat rarely makes people sick, so less "bad meat," and food cooked with clean water means less gut infection for children.
- **Food for the weak.** Soft cooked food lets small children wean and lets elders eat. It also gives the hearth a real role and creates a reason to gather firewood, which becomes a real task (and a winter burden).

I'd add it as a natural activity with stages that tie into the Fire skill (roasting known at start, then hearth cooking, then boiling with hot stones in sealed baskets, then pots). It fits the discovery web we already have.

## The real issue: winter famines

**The cause:** with the starting knowledge, this valley only supports about 15–25 people through winter. Bands founded at 30 get cut down by early-spring famines, then hold steady. The famines are real: the camp's food hits zero and everyone's reserves are gone. That's why starvation is still ~95% of deaths.

**Good:** an emergent carrying capacity, and exactly the pressure storage, drying, and cooking should relieve.

**Bad:**
- Real foragers mostly died of illness, not famine.
- Whole bands losing half their adults in one spring is harsh, and may feel frustrating to watch.

**Options:**

| Option | What it means |
|---|---|
| **A. Add cooking** (recommended regardless) | More energy from the same food. Likely raises carrying capacity meaningfully on its own |
| **B. Let bands winter elsewhere** | Real foragers often moved with the seasons. The band could leave for winter grounds beyond the valley, as animals already do. Realistic, but they'd be off-screen part of the year |
| **C. Start with a bit more winter know-how** | Basic food caches and freezing meat, which real Homo sapiens had. Changes the starting knowledge you approved |
| **D. Milder valley climate** | Shorter or softer winters. Simple, but makes the world less harsh |
| **E. Keep it** | Famine stays the main early pressure, and discoveries are how you escape it |

**My suggestion:** **A** for sure, then see where it lands. If famines are still dominant, add **C** in a minimal form (food caches only), since real people had that. I'd avoid D, because harsh winters are what make discoveries matter.

Which do you want? After that, the plan continues: culture as customs, couple separation, crowding reducible by institutions, the immunity and medicine additions (childhood immunity, inherited resistance, new diseases, healers and remedy rituals), then the discovery system.

### Claude (Assistant)

## How people really survived cold winters

Pre-contact New York (the Lenape, a few thousand years ago) survived on technology that's in our discovery web, not on what our band starts with:
- **Storage:** bark-lined pits full of dried corn, beans, and nuts (acorns, hickory, chestnut), plus dried and smoked fish from spring runs and dried venison.
- **Housing and clothing:** sturdy bark-covered houses with fires inside, tailored hide clothing, moccasins, snowshoes.
- **Mobility:** a seasonal round, moving to sheltered winter camps and hunting deer in the snow.
- **Farming** (late, around 1000 CE): the "Three Sisters."

The earliest people in that region (Ice Age caribou hunters ~13,000 years ago), and Ice Age Europeans in places like France and the Alps' edges (~20,000–12,000 years ago), survived with:
- **Fitted fur clothing** sewn with eyed bone needles
- **Frozen meat caches** and **bone grease** (boiling crushed bones for fat, a huge winter calorie source)
- **Fat lamps,** caves and rock shelters, and huge ranges they moved across

## Where our band sits in time

Our starting kit (fire, stone tools, spears, foraging, language; no needles, no storage) is roughly **early modern humans, ~50,000–70,000 years ago**. Those people mostly lived in **milder regions**. Humans only moved into truly cold lands *after* inventing fitted clothing, storage, and fat lamps. So a band like ours facing hard winters is like an early group pushing into a colder frontier, and **struggling there is historically accurate**. The way out is the exact chain of discoveries we designed. That's a good sign for the design.

## The bigger realization: our valley is too dense

Real foragers lived at roughly **0.01 to 1 person per km²**, with bands ranging across hundreds of km². Our valley is ~3 km × 3 km, and we're putting 30 people in it, which is **10 per km²**, far above anything real. No real habitat could feed that many through winter without farming. That's the true root of the famines, more than missing tech.

Options:
1. **Treat the valley as compressed:** one cell represents a much bigger area, so the land's productivity is scaled up. Simple, but every distance and travel time becomes abstract.
2. **The band ranges beyond the valley,** as animals and neighbors already do. Long foraging and hunting trips, plus a seasonal move, happen off the visible map. The most realistic option, but some of the band's life happens off-screen.
3. **A bigger world:** make the playable area much larger. More realistic, but much more to render and simulate.

My suggestion is **2**, possibly with a light touch of 1. The visible valley is the band's home base and favorite grounds, and the wider region feeds them in lean times. It also fits the multi-island future: the land beyond is real and contains neighbors.

## Cooking tiles and an interest

Yes, it deserves its own skill. Draft **Cooking & food processing**:

| Stage | Needs | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| **Roasting** (known at start) | fire | Meat and roots give more energy; fewer meat sicknesses |
| **Hot-stone cooking** | built hearths | Even, safer cooking |
| **Boiling** in hide or sealed baskets with hot stones | hot-stone cooking + scraped hide or pitch-sealed basket | Soups and soft food: **children wean earlier, elders eat better** |
| **Bone grease** | boiling + plenty of bones from hunts | **A big winter fat source** from what's usually thrown away. Real Ice Age survival tech |
| **Earth ovens** | hot-stone cooking + digging | Big batches; tough roots become good food |
| **Flatbread** | grinding slab + wild grain | Grain becomes a staple |
| **Pot cooking** | pottery | Stews, porridge, everything above made easy |

It also creates real tasks: **gathering firewood** (heavy in winter), and someone **tending the hearth and cooking**, which is time not spent foraging. That's a natural trade-off where a division of labor could emerge.

**Interest:** I'd call it **Hearth**. It's vague and warm in the way you described: cooking, preserving food (drying, storage, grinding), and keeping the home (tending the fire, upkeep of shelters). Fire would stay about mastering fire itself (high heat, torches, firing clay). Hearth-minded people become the band's cooks, food keepers, and homemakers, and probably its best defense against famine.

## What I need from you

1. **Space:** which option (1, 2, 3, or a mix)?
2. **Cooking skill** as drafted, and the **Hearth** interest: yes?

Once those are settled, I'll build cooking first, since it touches the food system we've been tuning, then go through the rest of the agreed list.

## Option 1: compressed scale

Each map cell stands for a bigger piece of land, say **250 m instead of 50 m**, so the same 64×64 map becomes a **16 km × 16 km** range (~250 km²).
- **Good:** realistic numbers for the land. At real forager densities (0.1–0.2 people/km²), that range supports about **25–50 people**, exactly real band sizes, so famines become occasional instead of constant. Animal numbers make sense too, and it costs nothing in performance.
- **Bad:** the picture and the clock disagree. A forager walks what *looks* like 200 m on screen and it takes 3 hours. Your camp sits next to "nearby" landmarks that are really kilometers away. It also gets awkward later, when villages, fields, and houses need fine detail around camp.

## Option 3: a bigger world

The map is actually as big as a band's range: **~20 km × 20 km** at real scale.
- **Good:**
  - Everything is genuinely there: walking 3 hours *looks* like 3 hours of walking, and neighbors live **on the same map**, visible and real.
  - It's what the multi-island future needs anyway (islands and mini-continents tens of km across).
  - It fits your "nothing off-world" goal.
- **Bad:**
  - More to simulate: at 50 m cells that's 160,000 cells vs. today's 4,000. Fine for the sim if plants regrow lazily, only when someone looks at a patch.
  - Unity has to render a 20 km landscape: terrain chunks loading and unloading, level of detail, camera travel. That's normal open-world technique, but real work.
  - Distant bands need cheaper "summary" simulation when you're not watching them, which we already planned for multi-society.

## What I'd suggest: a hybrid of the two

Make the **world real size (Option 3)**, but use **coarser cells for ecology**, say 200 m per cell for plants and animals, so 20 km is only ~100×100 cells, and render the terrain smoothly in Unity. Around camps and later villages, add **local detail** where it matters (individual huts, hearths, fields).

That gives:
- real distances and densities
- neighbors on the map, with nothing off-world
- reasonable performance
- the right foundation for islands later

Regional animal pools would also stop being an abstraction, since herds would really migrate across the visible land.

## Winter tech: mostly yes, already in the web

| Need | Already in the web |
|---|---|
| Shelter | Shelter skill: caves, lean-tos, huts, pit houses; Hide tents |
| Clothing | Hide & sewing: wrapped hides → fitted clothing with bone needles, plus footwear |
| Light and warmth | Fire: torches, stone fat lamps |
| Preserving food | Drying & smoking: racks, smoking |
| Storage | Storage: caches, lined pits, basket stores, raised caches |

**Worth adding:**
- **Cold caches:** burying meat in snow or frozen ground (Storage)
- **Pemmican:** dried meat pounded with fat, which keeps for a very long time and is extremely high-energy (Drying)
- **Snowshoes:** winter hunting and travel (Hide or Woodworking)
- **Bone grease:** from the cooking draft

**Important caveat:** none of these can be discovered yet, because the discovery system isn't built. The sim already reads some of them (clothing and caves raise cold protection), but no band can get them, which is part of why winters are so brutal right now. Building the discovery system fixes a lot of the winter problem on its own.

## Decisions

1. **World scale:** the hybrid (real-size world, coarser ecology cells), plain Option 1, or plain Option 3?
2. **Add** cold caches, pemmican, snowshoes, and the cooking skill + Hearth interest to the web?

**The hybrid keeps real densities and gives the band real space.** Plants and animals per km² stay at real-world levels. **Staging:** this beta's world becomes the band's real-size home range. Neighbor bands living *on* the map, with no abstract off-world region, comes with the multi-society phase, when the world grows to a region holding several bands.

The real-size world is in: the map is now 20 km × 20 km, with real walking distances for foragers, hunters, and herds. Tests pass (9/9), and the change is logged in `docs/PROGRESS.md`.

**Where things stand:**
- Bands now settle at **about 20–27 people** after some early famines, which is a realistic forager band size.
- **The limit isn't how much land there is.** Two things cap the population:
  - **The hungry gap in late spring:** winter stores are gone and new plants grow back slowly.
  - **Spoilage:** 20–30% of gathered food rots, because nothing preserves it yet.
- I also fixed a waste-of-time problem: people were making stone tools and fetching flint while starving. Now anything that isn't getting food fades when people are hungry.

1. **Cooking**, with its skill in the discovery web and the Hearth interest, plus cold caches, pemmican, snowshoes, and bone grease in the web.
2. **The discovery system:** experimenting, accidents, teaching, and losing knowledge, so preservation, clothing, shelter, healing, and cooking can actually be invented.
3. Then the rest of the list: culture as customs, couples separating, crowding easing through institutions, and the immunity and medicine additions.

### Claude (Assistant)

Here's the full walkthrough. I checked it against the current code, and it turned up a couple of things you should know about, which I've marked.

# 1. The loop

Every **hour**:
- each person's body burns energy: stomach first, then reserves, faster when working hard
- anyone who has finished what they were doing picks a new activity
- everyone advances their current activity by one hour

Every **day** (at midnight):
- plants regrow and animals move, breed, and hunt
- food spoils
- bodies update: hunger, cold, illness, healing, death
- births and conceptions
- families sleeping together exchange germs
- neighbors wander

# 2. How one person decides what to do

When someone is free, they list everything they *could* do right now, give each a score, add a little randomness, and pick the highest. The randomness keeps people from being perfectly predictable. Each score is built from what that person feels, knows, and is:

| Option | What its score is made of |
|---|---|
| **Eat** | How empty their belly is, plus how thin their body reserves are |
| **Rest** | Tiredness, poor health, injury, sickness |
| **Socialize** | Sociability, a bonus in the evening, *shrinks when hungry* |
| **Forage** | **Need** (how hungry they and the camp are, plus foresight about winter) × **what an hour of foraging has actually been bringing in** (learned) × a smaller pull from liking plants and patience |
| **Hunt** | Need (including craving meat) × **what an hour of hunting has actually been bringing in** × liking animals and strength, plus **prestige** (boldness × the band's valor), minus a penalty for late pregnancy or nursing an infant |
| **Knap / fetch flint** | How short the band is on tools, plus liking stone and knapping skill |
| **Care for child** | Warmth, plus how little care the child has had today |
| **Care for the sick** | Warmth, caring skill, healing interest, fondness for that person, how sick they are |
| **Watch & learn** (kids) | Curiosity, if an adult nearby is doing something to learn from |
| **Play** (kids) | A steady pull |

The key point: **the inputs are learned, so people adapt.** If hunting keeps failing, a person's expected return from hunting drops and they hunt less, without any rule telling them to. The same goes for foraging, specific places, specific animals' danger, and winter hardship.

# 3. What people learn (what feeds those scores)

Everything learned is stored as a belief that moves toward what actually happened:
- **Yield per hour** of foraging and hunting, from their own trips. Open people update faster; cautious people weigh disappointments double.
- **Danger of each animal:** rises fast after someone is hurt or killed, falls slowly after calm hunts. Witnessing a death teaches everyone, hardest those who loved the person.
- **Places:** how good each spot was, remembered per season.
- **How lean winter gets:** the *worst* of each winter, learned strongly after a famine.
- **Norms (valor, sharing):** from how risky hunts turn out, from famines, from good times.
- **Spreading:** beliefs pass between people in conversation, weighted toward charismatic and respected speakers, and children absorb the beliefs of adults they watch. That's how culture forms and drifts.

# 4. Group decisions

**Hunts:**
1. A person decides to hunt and asks others. **Each person compares joining with the best thing they'd otherwise do,** plus the pull of who's asking (fondness × the leader's charisma, and the leader's prestige).
2. **Choosing the animal:** each member values every animal in range for themselves: meat they'd share (only what can be eaten before it rots, scaled by hunger and party size) + prestige − fear × (how bad a wound would be + chance it kills, divided by how many share the risk) − walking distance on tired legs. Going home is always an option. Each person backs their favorite, the option with the most influence behind it wins, and people who strongly disagree and don't defer to the leader walk home.
3. **Keep going?** After each failure, injury, or wounded animal, everyone re-weighs with updated fear and tiredness. Darkness ends any hunt.

**Moving camp:** someone disappointed by recent foraging, who remembers a better place for this season, proposes it at the fire. Each adult weighs it by their own memory of that place, or by trust in the proposer if they've never been. Against it: attachment to the camp, tiredness from the last move, carrying small children and elders, and a fouled camp counts as a reason to go. Influence decides.

**Splitting the band:** after a long hard stretch, or when the camp is full of near-strangers, someone may propose leaving. People closest to them each weigh relief against the bonds they'd leave behind.

**Pairing:** fondness built through time together, plus attraction (partly cultural). A pair forms when it's mutual. If the couple spans two bands, whoever has less to lose moves.

# 5. How the waste-of-time fix works

**Honestly, it's one blanket rule:** when someone is hungry, every option *not* on an "essential" list (food work, eating, rest, care, talking) gets scaled down by up to 70%. So knapping and flint trips fade during hunger.

It works, but it's **a shortcut**, and I picked the list by hand. The truly natural version: making tools is valuable *because* tools make hunting work, so a starving person should value knapping by what it gets them **soon**, which is not much compared to food today. That requires people to value activities by what they ultimately lead to, which is a deeper change. I'd like to do it, but not before the discovery system.

# 6. How much food parents give their kids

**We don't track it, and digging in exposed a problem.** When a parent eats, they first fill every hungry child under 14 *completely* from the camp's food, with **no limit**, and both parents may each do this. Only then do they take their own portion, which *is* held back to a fair share in hard times. So in a famine, children get full portions while parents ration themselves. That's very likely why most starvation deaths are now adults.

**The natural fix:** in hard times, a **household** takes its fair share for everyone in it, children are fed first *within* that share, and how much parents sacrifice for their kids comes from their warmth. I'll fix this before the tech work.

# 7. Hard-coded audit: what's natural and what isn't

**Natural (emergent from people and their situation):** what each person does, who hunts, which animal, whether hunts continue, when the band moves, who pairs with whom and who moves, when bands split, what people fear, how cultures drift, who gets sick and from whom, who dies and why.

**But the "exchange rates" are hand-set.** Every score above contains numbers I chose: how strongly hunger pushes work, how much prestige matters against meat, how many rations an injury "costs," what fraction of fair share people take in hard times. They're the same for everyone. Individuals differ only through the traits and beliefs those numbers multiply. So decisions are **natural in structure** (who decides, based on what they know and feel), but **the strength of each motive is a designer choice.** Every agent sim has this. The way to keep it honest is:
- make more of these personal or cultural, as we did with the value of one's own life, and
- tune the species-wide ones against real data, like how many hours real foragers spend working, hunting, and socializing.

**Remaining fixed rules worth fixing:**
- **Sleep is fixed to 9pm–6am,** and food work can only *start* between 6am and 1pm. These should come from **daylight by season** (long summer days, short winter ones). That would also make winter naturally harder.
- **Age gates:** hunting from 14, foraging from 6, knapping from 12. They should come from body size, strength, and skill.
- **Fixed cutoffs:** hunting requires health > 0.6 and injury < 0.3; a pair forms at fondness 0.35; at least 12 must leave and 12 stay in a split; a turned-down split proposal waits a season.
- **Biology simplifications:** fertility ages 16–42, a 270-day pregnancy, no conception while nursing a baby under 2. Reasonable, but they could come from the body.

**World facts that need grounding:** gathering rates, spoilage, disease numbers, animal stats, plant density. These need calibrating against real data, which is the planned tuning tool's job.

# 8. Variation between worlds

8 new seeds, 80 years each:

| Seed | Pop | Valor | Sharing | Mammoth kills | Camp moves | Joined / left | Splits |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11 | 12 | 0.48 | 0.32 | 1 | 6 | 7 / 5 | 0 |
| 12 | 26 | 0.48 | 0.41 | 11 | 4 | 18 / 11 | 0 |
| 13 | 27 | 0.47 | 0.46 | 34 | 32 | 16 / 5 | 1 |
| 14 | 27 | 0.48 | 0.34 | 40 | 28 | 14 / 5 | 0 |
| 15 | 30 | 0.53 | 0.33 | 16 | 7 | 34 / 3 | 0 |
| 16 | 22 | 0.49 | 0.48 | 35 | 1 | 18 / 7 | 0 |
| 17 | 17 | 0.61 | 0.36 | 54 | 10 | 10 / 5 | 1 |
| 18 | 23 | 0.53 | 0.38 | 33 | 14 | 17 / 2 | 0 |

**Lots of variation in behavior:** one band killed a single mammoth in 80 years while another killed 54. One band moved camp once, another 32 times. Who married in and out varies a lot, and seed 17's band became noticeably more daring than the others (0.61).

**Little variation in culture and trajectory:** sharing stays in a narrow 0.32–0.48, valor clusters around 0.5, and every band ends up as roughly the same kind of society, a small mobile band of 12–30. You called it: without progression, worlds differ in **texture, not trajectory**.

Divergence will come from:
- discoveries, where different routes lead to different toolkits
- customs that lock in, like taboos, rituals, and residence rules
- richer culture dimensions
- later, your interventions

**Also notable:** the residence custom is very lopsided in most worlds (34 joined vs. 3 left in seed 15). Once early couples happen to settle one way, the custom reinforces it. That's realistic, and exactly the kind of thing that should vary more between bands.

# Before cooking

1. Fix **household food sharing** (parents feeding kids within a fair share, sacrifice set by warmth).
2. Make **sleep and work hours follow seasonal daylight**.
3. Then **cooking and the cold-weather tech**, and the discovery system.

Do 1 and 2 first, or go straight to tech?

### Claude (Assistant)

## Your questions

**What "cultural attraction" means right now:** honestly, it's crude.
- Each band gets a set of preference weights at founding ("we find strength, boldness, warmth… attractive"), drawn randomly.
- The only drift: each successful kill nudges the band toward admiring boldness and strength.
- It's held by the **band as a whole**, not by people, and it doesn't spread through conversation.

It should work like the norms: each person holds their own sense of what's admirable, shaped by who earns respect and what helped the band survive, and spread by talk. I'll fold it into the culture work.

**How fondness works:**

Each pair of people has a fondness from 0 to 1. It **grows** from:
- talking together (more for sociable people)
- caring for someone, or teaching and watching
- being family (it starts high for kin)
- growing up in the same band (a baseline)
- pairing up

But it **never fades and never drops.** No absence, no arguments, no betrayal, no theft. Every relationship only gets warmer, which is the core gap behind your strife idea below.

**Beliefs:** partly. People hold learned beliefs about how well foraging and hunting pay, how dangerous each animal is, how good each place is by season, how lean winters get, and the two norms. They learn them from experience and spread them by talk, and children absorb them.

Not built yet:
- **coincidence-based superstition**
- beliefs **about you, the god**
- beliefs about **other people** (trust)
- **customs and taboos**

Those are the belief-graph work still ahead.

## Your social system idea

You're describing something real and important: **division of labor built on trust, and strife when it fails.** How I'd do it:
- **Reputation:** people form beliefs about *each other's* competence: "Tal is a good hunter," "Asha always finds food." They learn these from results, the same way they learn about animals.
- **Roles emerge from trust:** when good hunters are out, others trust them and do their own work (foraging, tools, childcare). Everyone joins in only when things are dire. Specialists appear from skill plus reputation; nobody assigns roles.
- **Fondness becomes two-way and alive:** it fades without contact, and drops with conflict.
- **Strife under hardship:**
  - **Blame:** failed hunts during famine lower trust in those hunters.
  - **Hoarding and theft:** desperate people with low warmth, in a band with weak sharing norms, take more than their share. If noticed, fondness collapses and disputes start.
  - **Fights:** hot-tempered people in a dispute can come to blows, causing injuries and sometimes deaths. This also fills the "violence" slice of real deaths that's missing now.
- **Splits along fault lines:** groups form around who trusts and likes whom, not a fixed "12 leave / 12 stay."
  - Each person judges whether a group is viable enough for *them* to risk it. A bold person with weak ties might go with 5 people.
  - **Someone can strike out alone:** to join a neighbor band, or to wander and maybe come back later. Lone wanderers face real danger.

That's a big system. I'd build it as part of the culture work, since trust, norms, and customs are tightly linked. Does that shape sound right?

## Building now

1. **Household food sharing:** a household takes its fair share for everyone in it, kids are fed first *within* it, and how much a parent sacrifices depends on their warmth.
2. **Sleep follows daylight:** long summer days, short winter days. People stay up a few hours after dark by the fire, wake at sunrise, and start trips only if there's enough light left.
3. **Age gates from the body:** a growth curve times strength or dexterity decides when someone can forage, knap, or hunt, instead of fixed ages.
4. **Hunting cutoffs bend with need:** desperate people will hunt while hurt or tired.

### Claude (Assistant)

All four changes are in, and tests pass (9/9):
- **Sleep follows daylight.** Summer days run 5am–9pm and winter days 8am–4pm. People stay up about 3 hours by the fire after dark, sleep until sunrise, and only start a trip with 6+ hours of light left. Hunts end at dark.
- **No age gates.** A growth curve times strength or dexterity decides when someone can forage, knap, or hunt, and play fades as kids grow.
- **Hunting limits bend with desperation.** A starving band sends out people who'd normally stay home hurt or tired.
- **Household sharing** works as described: a parent's fair share covers the household, kids are fed first, and warmth decides the sacrifice.

**But it revealed the real structural problem.** Deaths of children aged 5–14 dropped to **0%**, while **adults are now 68% of deaths**, almost all starvation. In real famines, children and the old suffer most. The cause:
- All food sits in **one shared camp pile**.
- Children are in camp all day and eat whenever they're hungry.
- Adults spend the day out foraging and hunting (and with hungry kids at home, barely snack in the field).
- So adults come home to an empty pile.

**That isn't how real foragers handled food.** Generally:
- **Plant food belongs to the household that gathered it.** You feed your own family from it.
- **Big-game meat gets shared band-wide,** by custom.
- Between households, sharing happens when someone **asks**, and how freely depends on kinship, fondness, and the band's sharing norm.

This is also **the foundation of the social system you described.** Once food has owners:
- **Hoarding** and **theft** become real: taking from someone else's household store.
- **Trust** between households matters: who shares with whom, and who doesn't.
- **Blame and strife** under hardship have something concrete to be about.
- **Splits** form along real lines: households that share with each other vs. those that don't.

So **household food ownership plus the social fabric** (two-way, fading fondness; trust and reputation; roles that emerge from who's trusted; theft, disputes, and fights; splits along fault lines, including people striking out alone) is one connected piece of work.

**Your call on order:**
1. **Social fabric first** (households, trust, strife, dynamic splits). It fixes the famine distribution properly and is foundational, but it's a big build before any tech.
2. **Cooking and cold-weather tech first, as agreed.** That eases famine pressure so distribution matters less for now, and the social fabric comes right after.

I'd lean **1**, since everything after it (tech, culture, conflict) sits on top of who owns what and who trusts whom. But 2 is fine if you'd rather see progress on discoveries sooner.

### Claude (Assistant)

Going with the social fabric. Here's the plan, then I'll build it in pieces, testing as I go:
1. **Households own food.** Gatherers bring food home to their own household. Meat from hunts is split between the hunters' households and the rest of the band according to the band's sharing norm. Families eat from their own stores, and orphans get taken in by kin or the warmest adults.
2. **Asking for food (demand sharing).** A hungry person whose household is out asks another household. The giver weighs it by warmth, the sharing norm, fondness, kinship, trust, and how much they can spare. Giving builds trust; refusal breeds resentment.
3. **Living relationships.** Fondness fades without contact. **Trust** becomes its own thing, built by sharing and by reliable hunters, and lost through refusals, theft, and failed hunts in hard times. Hunters are recruited by trust in the leader.
4. **Strife.** Desperate people with low warmth, in a band that doesn't value sharing, may **steal** from a better-stocked household. If caught, there are grudges and **disputes**, and hot-tempered people can come to **blows**. That's real injuries and, rarely, deaths.
5. **Dynamic splits.** Groups form along fondness, trust, and grievance lines. Each person judges whether a group is big enough for *them* (bold people accept smaller groups), and someone can **strike out alone** to join a neighbor band, and **come back** later if their ties pull them home.
